#### Topic: The internet

<!-- group_key: listening-p2-set008 -->
<!-- audio_source_key: listening-p2-set008-audio -->
<!-- transcript_status: source_word_comments -->
<!-- word_comment_ids: 314,315,316,317 -->
<details class="audio-transcript">
<summary>Audio transcript</summary>

**Segment 1 — watch films**

A: "I am a big fan of newly released movies, but the cost of going to the cinema is too high. I'm just a student, so I can't afford to go to the cinema regularly to indulge in my hobby. Therefore, I think the internet is great because it allows me to watch movies for free at home. I save both the time of going to the cinema and a considerable amount of money."

<!-- word_comment_id: 314; author: Châu Phạm; anchor: watch films -->

**Segment 2 — communicate with friends**

B: "In today's digital age, the internet helps us access social media to stay constantly connected with friends. Especially since I have relatives mainly living abroad or in distant cities, the internet has been a great help for my family to stay in touch. We often video call each other to share daily stories."

<!-- word_comment_id: 315; author: Châu Phạm; anchor: communicate with friends -->

**Segment 3 — complete assignments**

C: "The internet is a great resource for research, finding materials, and useful information for studying. My friends and I often go to each other's houses to do homework together. Instead of spending hours trying to figure out how to do our homework, we quickly go online and find the answers."

<!-- word_comment_id: 316; author: Châu Phạm; anchor: complete assignments -->

**Segment 4 — find transport information**

D: "I see that people often use the internet to watch movies, read newspapers, quickly update the news, and so on. But I usually use the internet to find directions and public transport to my friends' and relatives' houses. I'm a forgetful person, and I often forget the way, even if I've been there a hundred times. So the internet helps me a lot in not getting lost and easily find nearby public transport."

<!-- word_comment_id: 317; author: Châu Phạm; anchor: find transport information -->

</details>

<!-- word_comment_id: 322; author: Châu Phạm; anchor: travel by bus -->

B: "I had to take driving lessons to be able to drive to work. My mother works along the same route, so we often drive to work together. Occasionally, she takes over the driving, and I'm happy because driving together gives us more time to chat."

#### Topic: New museum in town

<!-- group_key: listening-p2-set013 -->
<!-- audio_source_key: listening-p2-set013-audio -->
<!-- transcript_status: source_word_comments -->
<!-- word_comment_ids: 334,335,336,337 -->
<details class="audio-transcript">
<summary>Audio transcript</summary>

**Segment 1 — Finished the visit quickly**

Person A: I was quite excited to see the new museum in town, but when I arrived, I found it smaller than I expected. There were only a few rooms open to visitors, and some exhibitions were still being prepared. I walked through the main displays, took a few photos, and left after about thirty minutes. I thought the museum was interesting, but there was not enough to keep me there for a long time.

<!-- word_comment_id: 334; author: Châu Phạm; anchor: Finished the visit quickly -->

**Segment 2 — Got lost during the visit**

Person B: I liked the idea of having a new museum in our town, but my visit was a little frustrating. The building was quite large, and the signs were not very clear. I tried to find the history section, but I ended up walking around the same area several times. In the end, I had to ask a staff member for help because I could not find the correct room.

<!-- word_comment_id: 335; author: Châu Phạm; anchor: Got lost during the visit -->

**Segment 3 — Enjoyed the use of technology**

Person C: For me, the best thing about the new museum was the modern technology. Instead of just reading information on the walls, visitors could use touch screens, audio guides, and even virtual reality to learn about the exhibits. This made the visit much more interesting and enjoyable, especially for young people.

<!-- word_comment_id: 336; author: Châu Phạm; anchor: Enjoyed the use of technology -->

**Segment 4 — Wants to visit again**

Person D: I had a really good time at the new museum, although I could not see everything in one visit. Some parts were very crowded, and I missed one special exhibition because I arrived too late. I think the museum has a lot to offer, so I would definitely like to go back another day and spend more time there.

<!-- word_comment_id: 337; author: Châu Phạm; anchor: Wants to visit again -->

</details>

#### Topic: Visiting a new museum (2026)

<!-- group_key: listening-p2-set014 -->
<!-- audio_source_key: listening-p2-set014-audio -->
<!-- transcript_status: source_word_comments -->
<!-- word_comment_ids: 338,339,340,341 -->
<details class="audio-transcript">
<summary>Audio transcript</summary>

**Segment 1 — Finished the visit quickly**

I nearly postponed the museum trip because I had a dentist's appointment across town. Still, I thought I could squeeze in one gallery before leaving. I scanned the map, hurried past the café, and stopped only long enough to photograph the enormous whale skeleton. When the announcement said the next guided tour would begin in twenty minutes, I was already checking my watch. My friends stayed for the ancient coins exhibition, but I was back outside before they had even reached the second floor.

<!-- word_comment_id: 338; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: Finished the visit quickly -->

**Segment 2 — Got lost during the visit**

The building looked simple from outside, but inside it was a maze. I followed a blue arrow for "Local History," went through two glass doors, and somehow arrived back beside the dinosaur model. A guard pointed me upstairs, yet the lift opened in the modern art wing. By then, my sister had sent three messages asking where I was. I finally found her after asking at the information desk. I saw more corridors than exhibits that afternoon.

<!-- word_comment_id: 339; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: Got lost during the visit -->

**Segment 3 — Enjoyed the use of technology**

I expected rows of objects behind glass, so the museum surprised me. At one display, I moved a tablet over an old ship model and watched sailors appear on the screen. In another room, headphones recreated the sounds of a crowded nineteenth-century market. The paintings were impressive, of course, but I spent longest at the interactive tables, trying different languages and zooming into tiny details. That digital side of the museum turned what could have been a visit into something genuinely exciting.

<!-- word_comment_id: 340; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: Enjoyed the use of technology -->

**Segment 4 — Wants to visit again**

My cousin complained that the museum was too crowded, and she was right-we for the space exhibition and missed the final planetarium show. Even so, the few rooms we managed to enter were excellent. I kept noticing doors marked "Members only" and posters for an exhibition opening next month. On the bus home, I checked the museum website and saved the date. One afternoon clearly wasn't enough, so I've already persuaded my brother to come back with me.

<!-- word_comment_id: 341; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: Wants to visit again -->

</details>

#### Topic: Doing exercises (2026)

<!-- group_key: listening-p2-set015 -->
<!-- audio_source_key: listening-p2-set015-audio -->
<!-- transcript_status: source_word_comments -->
<!-- word_comment_ids: 342,343,344,345 -->
<details class="audio-transcript">
<summary>Audio transcript</summary>

**Segment 1 — Improve work performance**

At three o'clock, my concentration used to disappear. I'd reread the same email, make silly mistakes, and reach for another coffee. A colleague suggested using half my lunch break for a brisk walk, which sounded like a waste of time. After a week, though, I noticed I was finishing reports earlier and handling difficult clients more calmly. I haven't become a fitness enthusiast, but I protect that thirty-minute walk now because the rest of my working day goes so much better.

<!-- word_comment_id: 342; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: Improve work performance -->

**Segment 2 — Find exercise tiring**

My sister finishes a workout glowing and cheerful. I finish one looking as though I've missed the last bus in a thunderstorm. Ten minutes into a class, my legs feel heavy; by the end, even climbing the stairs to my flat seems unreasonable. I keep trying because my doctor says movement is important, and I admit I sleep well afterwards. But that famous burst of energy everyone promises? It has never arrived. Exercise simply drains me.

<!-- word_comment_id: 343; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: Find exercise tiring -->

**Segment 3 — Have fun exercising with others**

Last month, I joined a dance-fitness class only because my neighbour refused to go alone. Neither of us could follow the instructor at first, and we laughed so much that the people behind us started laughing too. Now six of us meet before class, share music, and celebrate whenever someone finally learns a difficult routine. I rarely notice how hard we're working until the session ends. The company, jokes, and friendly competition are what keep me coming back.

<!-- word_comment_id: 344; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: Have fun exercising with others -->

**Segment 4 — Hate exercising**

People keep offering me new ways to "discover the joy" of exercise: swimming, cycling, yoga, even boxing. I've tried enough of them to know the problem isn't the activity. I dislike changing into sports clothes, counting repetitions, sweating, and waking up with sore muscles. A walk to the bakery is fine because it has a useful destination, but exercising just for the sake of it feels like unpaid work. Give me a novel and a comfortable chair instead.

<!-- word_comment_id: 345; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: Hate exercising -->

</details>

#### Topic: Their protecting the environment (2026)

<!-- group_key: listening-p2-set016 -->
<!-- audio_source_key: listening-p2-set016-audio -->
<!-- transcript_status: source_word_comments -->
<!-- word_comment_ids: 346,347,348,349 -->
<details class="audio-transcript">
<summary>Audio transcript</summary>

**Segment 1 — protects the environment by using less electricity.**

My environmental habit began for a selfish reason: an electricity bill that was almost twice the usual amount. I bought a plug monitor and discovered the old television box was using power all night. Since then, lights go off when a room is empty, laundry waits until there is a full load, and chargers come out of the wall. The lower bill is welcome, but knowing the household is wasting far less energy matters just as much now.

<!-- word_comment_id: 346; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: protects the environment by using less electricity. -->

**Segment 2 — protects the environment by not driving to work**

The office car park is visible from my desk, and every morning it fills with one person in each car. I used to be part of that line. Then roadworks turned my twenty-minute journey into an hour, so I tried cycling along the canal instead. It was , cheaper, and surprisingly relaxing. I still own a car for weekend trips, but it stays at home on working days. That single change has cut most of my weekly fuel use.

<!-- word_comment_id: 347; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: protects the environment by not driving to work -->

**Segment 3 — protects the environment by shopping online.**

Going to five different shops every Saturday meant five receipts, several plastic bags, and a lot of driving. Now I keep a list during the week and place one online order from a company that sends groceries in reusable boxes. The delivery van is already serving the whole neighbourhood, so dozens of separate car journeys are avoided. I know internet shopping is not automatically green, especially with urgent single-item deliveries, but combining everything into one order works well for me.

<!-- word_comment_id: 348; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: protects the environment by shopping online. -->

**Segment 4 — protects the environment by using less water.**

During last summer's dry spell, our town asked everyone to reduce water use. I put a bucket in the shower while it warmed up and used that water on the balcony plants. It felt slightly ridiculous at first, yet the bucket filled faster than I expected. Now I also turn off the tap while brushing my teeth and run the dishwasher only when full. Those small routines continued long after the restrictions ended because wasting clean water no longer feels harmless.

<!-- word_comment_id: 349; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: protects the environment by using less water. -->

</details>

|---|---|

#### Topic: Doing arts (2026)

<!-- group_key: listening-p2-set017 -->
<!-- audio_source_key: listening-p2-set017-audio -->
<!-- transcript_status: source_word_comments -->
<!-- word_comment_ids: 350,351,352,353 -->
<details class="audio-transcript">
<summary>Audio transcript</summary>

**Segment 1 — Doing as a social activity**

Our neighbourhood mural started with one blank wall and a box of donated paint. On Saturday morning, children drew outlines, older residents suggested local landmarks, and a professional artist somehow fitted all the ideas together. I painted a terrible-looking tree, but nobody minded; someone turned it into part of a forest. By lunchtime, strangers were sharing brushes and sandwiches. The finished picture is lovely, yet the best part was watching people who had never spoken before create something side by side.

<!-- word_comment_id: 350; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: Doing as a social activity -->

**Segment 2 — Doing arts with children**

When I set up paints for my nieces, I used to demonstrate exactly what they should make. They ignored me, mixed every colour into purple, and turned the paper sideways halfway through. Their pictures were far more imaginative than my neat example. Now I simply cover the table, give them old shirts, and let them lead. We invent stories about each drawing while we work. Making art with them is messy, unpredictable, and easily the most entertaining part of my week.

<!-- word_comment_id: 351; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: Doing arts with children -->

**Segment 3 — Doing arts as part of the job**

People imagine my design job is just sitting around waiting for inspiration. Yesterday, however, I redrew a restaurant logo seven times because the client wanted it "friendlier but more serious." There are budgets, deadlines, meetings, and files that must be prepared perfectly for printing. I still enjoy choosing colours and turning a rough idea into a strong image, but it is not a private hobby. The artwork has to solve someone else's problem and be delivered on time.

<!-- word_comment_id: 352; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: Doing arts as part of the job -->

**Segment 4 — Doing arts alone**

A friend invited me to a weekend painting group, but conversation breaks my concentration. I like shutting the spare-room door, putting my phone face down, and working until I forget what time it is. No one asks what the picture means or suggests a brighter colour. Some evenings I produce nothing worth keeping, and that is fine because nobody is watching. The privacy gives me room to experiment, make mistakes, and follow an idea without explaining it.

<!-- word_comment_id: 353; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: Doing arts alone -->

</details>

#### Topic: Online shopping (Version 2) (2026)

<!-- group_key: listening-p2-set018 -->
<!-- audio_source_key: listening-p2-set018-audio -->
<!-- transcript_status: source_word_comments -->
<!-- word_comment_ids: 354,355,356,357 -->
<details class="audio-transcript">
<summary>Audio transcript</summary>

**Segment 1 — It saves time**

Thursday used to vanish into errands. I would leave work, in traffic, search three shops for one item, and reach home too late to cook. Now I order household basics from my phone while waiting for a meeting to start. Last week I bought printer ink in under two minutes, whereas finding the right cartridge in town once took an entire afternoon. Convenience matters, but the hours I get back are the real reason I shop this way.

<!-- word_comment_id: 354; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: It saves time -->

**Segment 2 — It is cheaper**

I wanted a pair of running shoes, but the sports shop price made me put them straight back on the shelf. That evening I found the same model online, added a seasonal code, and paid twenty-five pounds less. I usually check delivery costs and return policies because a supposed bargain can become expensive. Still, comparing several sellers on one screen makes it much easier to spot a genuine deal, and my budget benefits from that.

<!-- word_comment_id: 355; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: It is cheaper -->

**Segment 3 — Products are delivered**

Buying a bookshelf sounds simple until you try carrying one onto a crowded bus. I learned that lesson with a small desk and promised myself never again. These days, bulky orders arrive at the entrance to my building, and the driver even calls if I am not home. I could probably find some items slightly cheaper in town, but avoiding the lifting and transport is worth more to me. The doorstep service is the advantage I notice most.

<!-- word_comment_id: 356; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: Products are delivered -->

**Segment 4 — There are more choices**

My feet are an awkward size: one shop may have the right length but only in black, while another has bright colours in every size except mine. Online, I can search dozens of brands and filter by size, material, and style before I look at the price. Sometimes the amount of choice becomes overwhelming, and I open far too many tabs. Even so, I would rather compare twenty suitable pairs than settle for the only pair left on a local shelf.

<!-- word_comment_id: 357; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: There are more choices -->

</details>

#### Topic: Studying habit (2026)

<!-- group_key: listening-p2-set019 -->
<!-- audio_source_key: listening-p2-set019-audio -->
<!-- transcript_status: source_word_comments -->
<!-- word_comment_ids: 358,359,360,361 -->
<details class="audio-transcript">
<summary>Audio transcript</summary>

**Segment 1 — Prefer to study late at night**

At eight in the evening, our house is still noisy: dinner plates, phone calls, and my little brother practising the trumpet badly. Around eleven, everything changes. The doors close, traffic outside fades, and nobody interrupts to ask where a charger is. I make tea, spread my notes across the kitchen table, and often work until one. It is not the healthiest schedule, perhaps, but those late hours are when difficult ideas finally stay in my head.

<!-- word_comment_id: 358; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: Prefer to study late at night -->

**Segment 2 — Prefer to study at various places**

If I sit at the same desk for several days, my attention begins to wander. I use the library for essays, a lively café for reading, and the park when I need to memorise vocabulary aloud without disturbing anyone. Before exams, I sometimes book an empty classroom. Friends say changing location wastes time, but each place gives a different kind of energy. I choose the setting according to the task rather than having one permanent study spot.

<!-- word_comment_id: 359; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: Prefer to study at various places -->

**Segment 3 — Prefer to study with music**

Silence makes every tiny sound seem enormous-the clock, the fridge, someone closing a door upstairs. A low instrumental playlist covers those distractions without pulling me away from the page. Songs with lyrics are dangerous because I start following the words, so I save them for breaks. When the same study playlist begins, my brain seems to recognise that it is time to work. Headphones and music have become as necessary as my notebook.

<!-- word_comment_id: 360; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: Prefer to study with music -->

**Segment 4 — Prefer to study in a quiet place**

I once tried revising in a café because everyone said the background noise was relaxing. In forty minutes, I learned a stranger's weekend plans, watched four drinks being made, and completed half a paragraph. Now I use a small room at the back of the library where phones must be silent. There is no music, no conversation, and almost nothing to look at. It sounds dull, but that emptiness is exactly what lets me concentrate.

<!-- word_comment_id: 361; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: Prefer to study in a quiet place -->

</details>

#### Topic: Their journey to work (2026)

<!-- group_key: listening-p2-set020 -->
<!-- audio_source_key: listening-p2-set020-audio -->
<!-- transcript_status: source_word_comments -->
<!-- word_comment_ids: 362,363,364,365 -->
<details class="audio-transcript">
<summary>Audio transcript</summary>

**Segment 1 — Walk with friends**

At 7:40 each morning, three of us meet beside the bakery. The direct route to the office takes twenty minutes, although we often choose the riverside path and arrive five minutes later. One friend brings coffee, another gives us the latest neighbourhood news, and I mostly listen until I am properly awake. Rain occasionally sends us onto the bus, but walking together has become part of our friendship rather than simply a way of reaching work.

<!-- word_comment_id: 362; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: Walk with friends -->

**Segment 2 — Go by bus**

Driving would be faster before seven, but I am never organised enough to leave that early. By eight, the roads are blocked and parking near the office costs a fortune. The number 26 stops almost outside my flat, so I board with a travel card and read the news until my stop. It is not always comfortable-schoolchildren fill every seat on Mondays-but I can let someone else deal with the traffic.

<!-- word_comment_id: 363; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: Go by bus -->

**Segment 3 — Walk alone**

My colleague lives two streets away and keeps suggesting we travel together. The truth is that the forty-minute walk is the only part of my day. At home, two children need breakfast; at work, meetings begin the moment I enter. Between those worlds, I put my phone away and follow the canal path by myself. I sometimes greet dog walkers, but I do not arrange company. Solitude is the point of the journey.

<!-- word_comment_id: 364; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: Walk alone -->

**Segment 4 — Drive car**

The train station is closer than my garage, yet the train requires two changes and frequently leaves me standing in the rain. With my own vehicle, I can drop my daughter at school, carry equipment for work, and take a different road when traffic is bad. Fuel and parking are expensive, so I share the journey with a colleague twice a week. Even with those costs, having the car gives my complicated mornings the flexibility they need.

<!-- word_comment_id: 365; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: Drive car -->

</details>

#### Topic: Shopping online (2026)

<!-- group_key: listening-p2-set021 -->
<!-- audio_source_key: listening-p2-set021-audio -->
<!-- transcript_status: source_word_comments -->
<!-- word_comment_ids: 366,367,368,369 -->
<details class="audio-transcript">
<summary>Audio transcript</summary>

**Segment 1 — products are delivered**

After breaking my ankle, ordinary shopping became a military operation: crutches, taxis, and someone to carry the bags upstairs. A neighbour showed me how to order groceries, and two hours later a driver placed everything beside my kitchen counter. My ankle healed months ago, but the habit remained. I still visit markets for fresh fruit, yet washing powder, bottled drinks, and other heavy items come to me. Home delivery changed online shopping from an option into my default.

<!-- word_comment_id: 366; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: products are delivered -->

**Segment 2 — it is cheaper**

My brother buys the first thing he sees; I treat shopping like detective work. I leave an item in the basket, search for vouchers, compare three sites, and check whether postage cancels the saving. It paid off when I furnished my room: the lamp, chair, and shelves all cost less online than the local shop's sale prices. The process takes patience, but finding the lowest total feels like winning a small competition.

<!-- word_comment_id: 367; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: it is cheaper -->

**Segment 3 — it saves time**

I run a small bakery and finish after most shops have closed. Before internet shopping, Saturday morning was lost to buying packaging, cleaning supplies, and ingredients I had forgotten. Now I place orders after midnight while the ovens cool. It may not sound exciting, but getting those errands off my weekend has given me time to see friends again. The biggest benefit is not comfort or price; it is recovering hours that work used to steal.

<!-- word_comment_id: 368; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: it saves time -->

**Segment 4 — it has more choices**

I collect old film posters, which is a hopeless hobby if you only search local shops. Online marketplaces connect me with sellers in tiny towns and even other countries. One week I found ten versions of the same poster, each from a different cinema release. I have to inspect photographs carefully because condition varies, but the range is extraordinary. A physical shop might offer one lucky discovery; the internet gives me an entire world of possibilities.

<!-- word_comment_id: 369; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: it has more choices -->

</details>

#### Topic: Protecting the environment (2026)

<!-- group_key: listening-p2-set022 -->
<!-- audio_source_key: listening-p2-set022-audio -->
<!-- transcript_status: source_word_comments -->
<!-- word_comment_ids: 370,371,372,373 -->
<details class="audio-transcript">
<summary>Audio transcript</summary>

**Segment 1 — Shopping online**

My nearest large supermarket is twelve kilometres away. I used to make that journey whenever we ran out of one or two things, which was wasteful and expensive. Now the family adds items to a shared digital list, and I place one delivery order each week. The van already serves several houses on our road, so my separate car trips have almost vanished. I avoid express deliveries and combine purchases, making online ordering the greener option in our situation.

<!-- word_comment_id: 370; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: Shopping online -->

**Segment 2 — Not driving to work**

The final kilometre to my office used to take longer than the first ten because everyone was searching for parking. I tried the train, then discovered the station was close enough to cycle to. Now the car remains at home Monday to Friday. I arrive without traffic frustration, spend less on fuel, and get some movement before sitting at a desk. One commuting decision solved three problems at once.

<!-- word_comment_id: 371; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: Not driving to work -->

**Segment 3 — Using less electricity**

Our smart meter became an unexpected family game. The children run around checking which light was left on, and I compare today's energy use with yesterday's. We lowered the heating slightly, stopped using the tumble dryer on sunny days, and replaced two ancient bulbs. The numbers on the screen drop immediately, which makes the effect visible. What began as a way to reduce bills has turned into a household effort to consume less power.

<!-- word_comment_id: 372; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: Using less electricity -->

**Segment 4 — Using less water**

I noticed the garden hose was using drinking water to do a job rain could handle. We installed a barrel beneath the roof gutter, and one storm filled it completely. That supply now waters the vegetables and cleans muddy tools. Inside, I take shorter showers and repair dripping taps Water once seemed endless because it always arrived when I turned a handle; collecting and measuring it changed that attitude.

<!-- word_comment_id: 373; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: Using less water -->

</details>

#### Topic: Running

<!-- group_key: listening-p2-set023 -->
<!-- audio_source_key: listening-p2-set023-audio -->
<!-- transcript_status: source_word_comments -->
<!-- word_comment_ids: 374,375,376,377 -->
<details class="audio-transcript">
<summary>Audio transcript</summary>

**Segment 1 — prefer running in the street**

A treadmill tells you exactly what will happen: the belt moves, the wall stays still, and every minute feels identical. Outside, the city keeps changing. I weave past commuters, climb the hill by the theatre, and use shop windows to check my posture. Traffic means I have to stay alert, so I choose roads and run early. The pavement is not perfect, but the movement and unpredictability of the streets keep me interested.

<!-- word_comment_id: 374; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: prefer running in the street -->

**Segment 2 — at the seaside**

I time my run for low tide. The sand near the water becomes firm enough to move , while the softer parts make my legs work harder. Waves cover the noise of my breathing, gulls circle overhead, and there is always a cool breeze on the return journey. I own a gym membership for rainy days, but nothing indoors matches the feeling of finishing beside the sea and walking straight into the water.

<!-- word_comment_id: 375; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: at the seaside -->

**Segment 3 — on the running track**

Before a race, guessing distance is useless. I need to know whether my fourth kilometre was slower than my first, so I train at the stadium. Each lap is measured, the surface is even, and my coach can watch without cycling behind me. It can feel repetitive, especially after twenty laps, but that repetition reveals every weakness in my pace. For serious sessions, the track gives me information no scenic route can provide.

<!-- word_comment_id: 376; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: on the running track -->

**Segment 4 — in the fitness center**

Winter defeated my outdoor routine. Darkness arrived before I left work, rain soaked my shoes, and icy paths made speed training unsafe. I joined the centre opposite my office and discovered I could run, stretch, and shower before going home. The treadmill is less beautiful than the park, but I can control the incline and never cancel because of weather. During the week, that reliable indoor setup is exactly what I need.

<!-- word_comment_id: 377; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: in the fitness center -->

</details>

#### Topic: Their purposes of using the Internet (2026)

<!-- group_key: listening-p2-set024 -->
<!-- audio_source_key: listening-p2-set024-audio -->
<!-- transcript_status: source_word_comments -->
<!-- word_comment_ids: 378,379,380,381 -->
<details class="audio-transcript">
<summary>Audio transcript</summary>

**Segment 1 — watch film**

Friday night is my private cinema night. I dim the lights, make popcorn, and search streaming sites for something I have not seen. Sometimes I read reviews for twenty minutes and then choose an old favourite anyway. I use email for work and social media occasionally, but those feel like chores. The part of the internet I genuinely look forward to is having thousands of films available without leaving the sofa.

<!-- word_comment_id: 378; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: watch film -->

**Segment 2 — communicate with friends**

Our friendship group now lives in four countries. Meeting in person requires months of planning, yet on Sunday evenings we all open the same video-call link. Someone cooks, someone is still commuting, and one friend usually forgets the time difference. We share ordinary details that would never justify an international phone call. For me, online access matters most because it keeps distant friendships feeling like everyday relationships.

<!-- word_comment_id: 379; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: communicate with friends -->

**Segment 3 — complete school assignments**

The school library has excellent books, but they close at five and my best working time starts after dinner. Online databases let me find journal articles, check facts, and watch explanations when a textbook makes no sense. I also upload finished work through the class portal before midnight. Entertainment sites are tempting, so I use an app that blocks them for an hour. Without the internet, completing coursework from home would be far more difficult.

<!-- word_comment_id: 380; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: complete school assignments -->

**Segment 4 — find transport information**

I can remember a face for years and forget a bus number in five minutes. Before leaving anywhere, I check the route online, save the platform, and take a screenshot in case the signal disappears. Real-time updates have rescued me from cancelled trains more than once. Friends use the internet mainly for messages and videos; I open it most often when I need to know how to get somewhere and when the next service leaves.

<!-- word_comment_id: 381; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: find transport information -->

</details>

#### Topic: When they like listening to music (2026)

<!-- group_key: listening-p2-set025 -->
<!-- audio_source_key: listening-p2-set025-audio -->
<!-- transcript_status: source_word_comments -->
<!-- word_comment_ids: 382,383,384,385 -->
<details class="audio-transcript">
<summary>Audio transcript</summary>

**Segment 1 — To relax**

There is a particular moment after work when conversation feels impossible. I put my bag down, lower the lights, and play something slow-usually piano or acoustic guitar. I do not sing along or answer messages; I simply let the day become Music during exercise is too distracting for me, and mornings need silence. Its real value comes in that evening half-hour when my shoulders finally drop and my thoughts stop racing.

<!-- word_comment_id: 382; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: To relax -->

**Segment 2 — While studying**

My revision playlist has no words and, according to my brother, no memorable tunes either. That is intentional. A steady background sound stops me checking every noise in the house, but lyrics would steal my attention from the page. I press play when I open my notes and stop it during breaks. Over time, those tracks have become a signal: once they begin, I settle down and work.

<!-- word_comment_id: 383; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: While studying -->

**Segment 3 — While singing**

I do not listen passively for long. The moment a chorus arrives, I am holding a hairbrush like a microphone and trying to copy the singer's breathing. I slow difficult sections down, repeat them, and record myself to hear where the notes go wrong. On the bus, I prefer podcasts, and while studying I need Songs matter most when I can use them to practise my own voice.

<!-- word_comment_id: 384; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: While singing -->

**Segment 4 — After waking up**

My alarm is technically music because an ordinary beep puts me in a terrible mood. The first song starts softly, then the playlist becomes brighter while I open the curtains and make breakfast. By the time the third track finishes, I am usually dressed. I rarely play music late at night; it keeps me awake. Its most useful job is pulling me out of sleep and giving the morning some energy.

<!-- word_comment_id: 385; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: After waking up -->

</details>

#### Topic: Protecting the environment (Version 2) (2026)

<!-- group_key: listening-p2-set026 -->
<!-- audio_source_key: listening-p2-set026-audio -->
<!-- transcript_status: source_word_comments -->
<!-- word_comment_ids: 386,387,388,389 -->
<details class="audio-transcript">
<summary>Audio transcript</summary>

**Segment 1 — Does not use commercial cleaning products**

The supermarket cleaning aisle smells so strong that it makes my eyes water. Worse, every product comes in another plastic bottle. I began making one simple spray from vinegar, water, and citrus peel, expecting it to be a temporary experiment. It cleaned the kitchen surprisingly well, so I added baking soda for tougher jobs. I still buy soap, of course, but the specialised chemical cleaners have disappeared from my cupboard and from my shopping list.

<!-- word_comment_id: 386; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: Does not use commercial cleaning products -->

**Segment 2 — Give away used items**

My wardrobe was full, yet I wore the same ten things. Rather than throw the rest away, I organised a free clothes table at our community centre. A teenager chose my old winter coat, and another family took bedding we no longer needed. They later brought toys for someone else to collect. Passing things on creates a useful circle: one person's clutter becomes another person's solution, with no new product needing to be made.

<!-- word_comment_id: 387; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: Give away used items -->

**Segment 3 — Buy environmentally friendly products**

I cannot control how every factory operates, but I can decide which ones receive my money. Before buying, I now check whether packaging is recyclable, materials are responsibly sourced, and replacement parts are available. Last month I chose a repairable kettle over a cheaper sealed model. It may last longer and create less waste. Shopping this way takes more thought, but it turns an ordinary purchase into support for better production.

<!-- word_comment_id: 388; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: Buy environmentally friendly products -->

**Segment 4 — Reuse containers for storing food**

Sunday cooking once ended with a bin full of tubs and jars. I started washing them instead: yoghurt pots hold chopped herbs, glass jars store soup, and takeaway containers carry lunches to work. I label everything because frozen tomato sauce and strawberry puree look alarmingly similar. The system saves money and reduces single-use packaging. More importantly, it makes me pause before treating a perfectly useful container as rubbish.

<!-- word_comment_id: 389; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: Reuse containers for storing food -->

</details>

|---|---|

#### Topic: Protecting the environment (Version 3) (2026)

<!-- group_key: listening-p2-set027 -->
<!-- audio_source_key: listening-p2-set027-audio -->
<!-- transcript_status: source_word_comments -->
<!-- word_comment_ids: 390,391,392,393 -->
<details class="audio-transcript">
<summary>Audio transcript</summary>

**Segment 1 — Give away used items**

When we moved, I counted six lamps, three chairs, and boxes of clothes we no longer used. Hiring a rubbish collection would have been easiest, but most of it was perfectly good. I posted photographs in a neighbourhood group, and by Sunday everything had new owners, including a student delighted with the old desk. Nothing earned me money, yet seeing useful objects leave in people's hands rather than a truck felt far better.

<!-- word_comment_id: 390; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: Give away used items -->

**Segment 2 — Buy environmentally friendly products**

I used to choose shampoo by smell and washing-up liquid by price. Then our local river was closed after pollution levels rose, and I started reading labels. Now I look for refillable bottles, recycled packaging, and ingredients that break down safely. These products sometimes cost a little more, so I buy fewer of them and use them carefully. My shopping basket is a small thing, but it supports companies making less damaging choices.

<!-- word_comment_id: 391; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: Buy environmentally friendly products -->

**Segment 3 — Reuse containers for storing food**

My grandmother never threw away a glass jar; I used to tease her cupboard full of them. Now my kitchen looks remarkably similar. Coffee jars hold rice, takeaway boxes carry lunches, and small containers organise herbs in the freezer. Reusing them means I buy fewer plastic bags and can actually see what food I have, so less gets forgotten. Grandmother calls it common sense, not environmentalism, and perhaps she is right.

<!-- word_comment_id: 392; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: Reuse containers for storing food -->

**Segment 4 — Does not use commercial cleaning products**

The warning labels on bathroom cleaners began to bother me-open the window, wear gloves, keep away from children. I wondered why something used in my home needed so many hazards. After some experiments, I found that diluted vinegar handles glass, baking soda cleans the sink, and lemon removes unpleasant smells. I no longer bring home rows of brightly coloured chemical bottles. The homemade mixtures are simpler, cheaper, and much kinder to what goes down the drain.

<!-- word_comment_id: 393; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: Does not use commercial cleaning products -->

</details>

#### Topic: Online shopping (Version 2) (2026)

<!-- group_key: listening-p2-set028 -->
<!-- audio_source_key: listening-p2-set028-audio -->
<!-- transcript_status: source_word_comments -->
<!-- word_comment_ids: 394,395,396,397 -->
<details class="audio-transcript">
<summary>Audio transcript</summary>

**Segment 1 — The products are delivered**

My apartment is on the fifth floor and the lift has been broken twice this year. Carrying a month's supply of pet food upstairs convinced me to order it online. The courier brings the heavy box to my door, and I no longer plan shopping around who can help me lift it. I still enjoy browsing local shops for small things, but for regular bulky purchases, the arrival at home is the feature that wins.

<!-- word_comment_id: 394; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: The products are delivered -->

**Segment 2 — It is cheaper**

I keep a spreadsheet for major purchases, which my friends find excessive. It includes the shop price, online price, delivery fee, and any cashback. When I bought headphones, the first website looked cheapest until postage was added; another seller offered a code and free delivery. The final saving paid for a concert ticket. Online shopping rewards careful comparison, and that lower total is why I use it.

<!-- word_comment_id: 395; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: It is cheaper -->

**Segment 3 — It saves time**

During exam season, leaving campus for shopping breaks my whole rhythm. I might spend forty minutes travelling to town just to discover the item is unavailable. Ordering online takes a few minutes between study sessions, and I can collect the parcel from reception later. Delivery is convenient, but the important difference is what does not disappear: an afternoon that I can use for revision, cooking, or sleep.

<!-- word_comment_id: 396; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: It saves time -->

**Segment 4 — There are more choices**

Local furniture shops assume everyone wants grey, white, or brown. I was searching for a narrow green chair that would fit beside a bookshelf, and no showroom had one. Online filters produced hundreds, including different fabrics, leg styles, and exact measurements. Too many options can delay a decision, but at least the right item exists somewhere. That breadth matters more to me than discounts or next-day delivery.

<!-- word_comment_id: 397; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: There are more choices -->

</details>

#### Topic: Listening to music (2026)

<!-- group_key: listening-p2-set029 -->
<!-- audio_source_key: listening-p2-set029-audio -->
<!-- transcript_status: source_word_comments -->
<!-- word_comment_ids: 398,399,400,401 -->
<details class="audio-transcript">
<summary>Audio transcript</summary>

**Segment 1 — To relax**

My train home is crowded, but headphones create a small private room around me. I choose slow music, look out of the window, and let conversations fade into the background. By my stop, the day's problems feel less urgent. I cannot use music while reading because I follow the melody instead of the words, and loud morning songs irritate me. I save it for the moments when I need to unwind.

<!-- word_comment_id: 398; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: To relax -->

**Segment 2 — After waking up**

Before I discovered morning playlists, I pressed the snooze button three or four times. Now the speaker starts with a song I love at seven, and I have to cross the room to turn it up. I dance badly while making coffee, which is ridiculous but effective. At other times of day I may prefer silence; immediately after sleep, however, music does the job that alarms and motivation never managed.

<!-- word_comment_id: 399; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: After waking up -->

**Segment 3 — While singing**

Our family karaoke machine was meant for parties, but I use it nearly every evening. I play the original track once, then remove the lead vocal and try the song myself. Sometimes I send a recording to my choir teacher for feedback. Background music while working only distracts me, so I do not keep it on casually. I listen closely when I am about to sing, because the song becomes a lesson.

<!-- word_comment_id: 400; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: While singing -->

**Segment 4 — While studying**

A silent study room makes me aware of every page turning. A crowded room is worse. Soft electronic music through headphones sits between those extremes and creates a steady wall of sound. I avoid familiar songs because memories come with them, and I never use shuffle-the sudden change breaks my focus. That carefully chosen music accompanies essays, revision, and almost every long assignment I complete.

<!-- word_comment_id: 401; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: While studying -->

</details>

#### Topic: Outdoor activities (2026)

<!-- group_key: listening-p2-set030 -->
<!-- audio_source_key: listening-p2-set030-audio -->
<!-- transcript_status: source_word_comments -->
<!-- word_comment_ids: 402,403,404,405 -->
<details class="audio-transcript">
<summary>Audio transcript</summary>

**Segment 1 — horse riding**

The horse I ride is called Pepper, and she knows the route better than I do. We leave the stable, cross a shallow stream, and follow a path through the fields. Cycling is faster and hiking is easier to organise, but neither involves learning to communicate with an animal that has its own mood and opinions. That partnership is what keeps me returning to the riding centre every weekend.

<!-- word_comment_id: 402; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: horse riding -->

**Segment 2 — going for a run**

My job keeps me indoors under artificial light, so after work I need movement and open sky. I lace up beside the office and follow a five-kilometre route around the lake. Some days I run ; on others I slow down and watch the birds. I tried joining a gym, but staring at a wall while exercising felt wrong. Running outside clears my head in a way no machine has managed.

<!-- word_comment_id: 403; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: going for a run -->

**Segment 3 — hiking**

After my mountain-bike accident, I was reluctant to return to steep trails. I still missed the views, though, so a friend suggested reaching them on foot. Walking uphill takes longer and lacks the rush of cycling, but I notice wildflowers, old paths, and places I used to race past. The mountains are still part of my weekends; I simply explore them now with boots and a backpack.

<!-- word_comment_id: 404; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: hiking -->

**Segment 4 — mountain biking**

Horse riding looks elegant until a large horse turns its head toward me; then I remember how nervous animals make me. My friends and I prefer machines we can repair ourselves. We load our bikes onto a van, climb forest tracks, and race down muddy slopes. It is demanding, occasionally frightening, and guaranteed to cover us in dirt. That combination is exactly why mountain biking appeals to me.

<!-- word_comment_id: 405; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: mountain biking -->

</details>

#### Topic: Places to run (2026)

<!-- group_key: listening-p2-set031 -->
<!-- audio_source_key: listening-p2-set031-audio -->
<!-- transcript_status: source_word_comments -->
<!-- word_comment_ids: 406,407,408,409 -->
<details class="audio-transcript">
<summary>Audio transcript</summary>

**Segment 1 — in the fitness centre**

The weather forecast used to decide whether I exercised. Heat, rain, or darkness each provided a good excuse. Then I joined a centre near the station. Inside, I can choose a treadmill, set a precise pace, and move straight to strength training afterwards. I miss outdoor scenery sometimes, but consistency matters more. A controlled indoor space means the session happens regardless of what is falling from the sky.

<!-- word_comment_id: 406; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: in the fitness centre -->

**Segment 2 — at the seaside**

I leave my shoes beside the door and start just before sunrise. The promenade is nearly empty, fishing boats are returning, and the air smells of salt. On calm days I run on firm sand; when the tide is high, I use the path above the beach. Hills might improve my fitness more , but the sea makes me want to go out, and that is what sustains a routine.

<!-- word_comment_id: 407; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: at the seaside -->

**Segment 3 — in the street**

There is a park fifteen minutes away, yet travelling there makes a short run feel like an expedition. I prefer stepping outside and beginning immediately. My route uses residential roads, a shopping street before it gets busy, and a hill near the station. I wear reflective clothing and avoid rush hour because cars are the obvious disadvantage. Even so, the convenience of running through my own streets keeps the habit simple.

<!-- word_comment_id: 408; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: in the street -->

**Segment 4 — on the running track**

I am training for ten kilometres and need honest numbers. GPS can lose signal beneath trees, but the stadium lane never changes: twenty-five laps means ten kilometres. The surface is forgiving, there are no traffic lights, and I can leave water beside the start line. Scenic routes are more enjoyable, but when I am testing pace or racing a friend, measured laps on the track are far more useful.

<!-- word_comment_id: 409; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: on the running track -->

</details>

#### Topic: Doing exercise (2026)

<!-- group_key: listening-p2-set032 -->
<!-- audio_source_key: listening-p2-set032-audio -->
<!-- transcript_status: source_word_comments -->
<!-- word_comment_ids: 410,411,412,413 -->
<details class="audio-transcript">
<summary>Audio transcript</summary>

**Segment 1 — hate exercising**

My flatmate has signed me up for three trial classes this year. I left spinning after twelve minutes, spent yoga watching the clock, and discovered that boxing gloves smell terrible. She says I simply haven't found the right activity. I think I have found the right conclusion. I understand the health arguments, but forcing myself through a workout makes me miserable. I would rather walk somewhere useful than exercise because a schedule tells me to.

<!-- word_comment_id: 410; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: hate exercising -->

**Segment 2 — help improve work performance**

On days when I go straight from bed to my desk, even simple decisions feel slow. A short swim before work changes that. By nine, I am alert, patient in meetings, and much less likely to postpone difficult tasks. The benefit surprised me because I originally joined the pool for my back. My posture improved, but the real reason I keep the early alarm is how much sharper and more productive the working day becomes.

<!-- word_comment_id: 411; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: help improve work performance -->

**Segment 3 — find exercise tiring**

Everyone at the running club talks about the wonderful feeling afterwards. Afterwards, I am usually lying on the floor wondering who moved the stairs farther away. Even gentle sessions leave my muscles heavy for the rest of the day. I have learned to exercise in the evening so I can sleep rather than struggle through work. I continue for health reasons, but energy is certainly not what it gives me.

<!-- word_comment_id: 412; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: find exercise tiring -->

**Segment 4 — has fun when exercising with friends**

I used to avoid the gym because rows of silent people on machines felt depressing. Then colleagues invited me to their Thursday circuit. We complain together, invent ridiculous team names, and celebrate finishing each round as though we won a championship. The exercises themselves are still hard, yet the hour passes because someone is always joking or encouraging me. Friendship turned a duty into an event I genuinely anticipate.

<!-- word_comment_id: 413; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: has fun when exercising with friends -->

</details>

#### Topic: The Internet (2026)

<!-- group_key: listening-p2-set033 -->
<!-- audio_source_key: listening-p2-set033-audio -->
<!-- transcript_status: source_word_comments -->
<!-- word_comment_ids: 414,415,416,417 -->
<details class="audio-transcript">
<summary>Audio transcript</summary>

**Segment 1 — watch films**

Cinema tickets in my town now cost almost as much as dinner. I still go for big releases with friends, but most evenings I use a streaming service and connect the laptop to our old projector. My brother makes popcorn, we argue about what to choose, and the living room becomes our private cinema. I use the internet for plenty of practical things, yet watching films is the activity that takes most of my free time online.

<!-- word_comment_id: 414; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: watch films -->

**Segment 2 — communicate with friends**

My grandmother refused video calls at first because she thought the camera would stay on forever. Now she calls every Wednesday and carries the tablet around the garden to show me new flowers. Messages are useful, but seeing her face makes the distance feel smaller. News, shopping, and entertainment are all available online; none matters to me as much as maintaining conversations with people I cannot visit often.

<!-- word_comment_id: 415; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: communicate with friends -->

**Segment 3 — complete assignments**

Our history teacher gave us a project on a building that no longer exists. The textbook offered one paragraph, but online archives had maps, newspaper reports, and photographs from several decades. My group shared links in a document and built the presentation without meeting in person. Of course, we had to check reliable sources rather than copy the first result. The internet's main role in my life is helping turn school tasks into completed work.

<!-- word_comment_id: 416; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: complete assignments -->

**Segment 4 — find transport information**

A cancelled bus once left me outside a station for ninety minutes because I had not checked the service notice. Since then, I open a journey-planning app before every unfamiliar trip. It compares trains and buses, shows walking connections, and warns me when a platform changes. I rarely use social media, and films are easier on television. Online transport information, however, prevents real problems, so that is what I rely on most.

<!-- word_comment_id: 417; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: find transport information -->

</details>

#### Topic: Art (2026)

<!-- group_key: listening-p2-set034 -->
<!-- audio_source_key: listening-p2-set034-audio -->
<!-- transcript_status: source_word_comments -->
<!-- word_comment_ids: 418,419,420,421 -->
<details class="audio-transcript">
<summary>Audio transcript</summary>

**Segment 1 — social activity**

I joined a printmaking workshop without knowing anyone. By the end of the first evening, four of us were sharing ink, comparing mistakes, and planning a small exhibition in the café next door. The prints were individual, but the process was wonderfully communal; one person's failed pattern often inspired another person's idea. I enjoy the objects we produce, yet the real attraction is having a reason to gather, talk, and make something with other people.

<!-- word_comment_id: 418; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: social activity -->

**Segment 2 — with children**

Children never ask whether blue trees are realistic. They paint them, add orange rain, and explain an entire world before an adult has chosen a pencil. I volunteer at a Saturday club, laying out materials and helping with scissors, but I try not to control the result. Their confidence reminds me to take art less seriously. Working beside them is noisy and chaotic, and it constantly refreshes my own imagination.

<!-- word_comment_id: 419; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: with children -->

**Segment 3 — as part of their job**

Every chapter of the educational book I edit needs illustrations. I sketch diagrams, discuss changes with the writer, and adjust colours so they remain clear when printed. Sometimes I would rather draw whatever interests me, but the image must support the text and meet a deadline. Art is woven into my working week, complete with feedback and revisions. It is creative, certainly, but it also has a practical purpose and a client.

<!-- word_comment_id: 420; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: as part of their job -->

**Segment 4 — alone**

Group workshops make me compare every line with the person beside me. At home, that pressure disappears. I put on an old shirt, clear the kitchen table, and draw for hours without showing anyone. Some pages are ugly experiments that go straight into a drawer. That secrecy is valuable: I can change direction, abandon an idea, or make something deeply personal without an audience waiting for an explanation.

<!-- word_comment_id: 421; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: alone -->

</details>

#### Topic: Transportation to work (2026)

<!-- group_key: listening-p2-set035 -->
<!-- audio_source_key: listening-p2-set035-audio -->
<!-- transcript_status: source_word_comments -->
<!-- word_comment_ids: 422,423,424,425 -->
<details class="audio-transcript">
<summary>Audio transcript</summary>

**Segment 1 — travel by bus**

The bus is not glamorous, but it is predictable. The stop sits opposite my building, the route ends beside the office, and a monthly pass costs less than parking for a week. I usually stand during the first ten minutes, then find a seat after the school stop. Cycling would be healthier, though the main road feels unsafe. Letting the bus carry me through the morning traffic is the practical choice I make every day.

<!-- word_comment_id: 422; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: travel by bus -->

**Segment 2 — travel by car**

My work involves visiting two sites and carrying a case of testing equipment. Public transport can reach each place, but not in the order I need, and the equipment is awkward on a crowded train. Driving lets me leave early, change plans, and collect my son on the return journey. I dislike fuel prices and avoid unnecessary trips, yet for this particular routine, the car is less a luxury than a mobile tool.

<!-- word_comment_id: 423; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: travel by car -->

**Segment 3 — walk alone**

Moving closer to the office changed my mornings completely. Instead of checking traffic reports, I step out with a coffee and follow a street for fifteen minutes. Colleagues sometimes offer lifts, but I decline unless the weather is extreme. Walking by myself gives me time to organise my thoughts before conversations begin. It is a short journey, though the silence between home and work has become surprisingly important.

<!-- word_comment_id: 424; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: walk alone -->

**Segment 4 — walk with a friend**

Maya and I discovered we lived on neighbouring roads after complaining about the same delayed bus. Now we meet at the corner and walk the final three kilometres together. She sets a pace; I remember umbrellas and bring coffee. On difficult mornings, one of us would probably choose the bus alone, but neither wants to disappoint the other. The shared journey keeps us active and turns commuting into regular catch-up time.

<!-- word_comment_id: 425; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: walk with a friend -->

</details>

#### Topic: Studying (Version 2) (2026)

<!-- group_key: listening-p2-set036 -->
<!-- audio_source_key: listening-p2-set036-audio -->
<!-- transcript_status: source_word_comments -->
<!-- word_comment_ids: 426,427,428,429 -->
<details class="audio-transcript">
<summary>Audio transcript</summary>

**Segment 1 — In various places**

My concentration depends more on the task than the address. I read theory on the train, write essays in the library, review flashcards in a café, and practise presentations while walking through the park. Staying in one place too long makes me restless, whereas changing surroundings gives each activity a fresh start. I carry a small charger and headphones, so almost any table or bench can become a temporary study area.

<!-- word_comment_id: 426; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: In various places -->

**Segment 2 — In a quiet place**

My friends revise together and claim the discussion helps. In our sessions, discussion usually becomes gossip within ten minutes. I work better in the silent section upstairs, where even unwrapping food earns angry looks. With no conversation to follow and no music playing, I can hold a complicated argument in my head. I meet friends after studying, but the learning itself needs a place with almost no noise.

<!-- word_comment_id: 427; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: In a quiet place -->

**Segment 3 — With music**

I created one playlist for each subject during my first year. Piano means mathematics, gentle electronic music means reading, and film soundtracks mean essay writing. The categories are unnecessary, perhaps, but the routine settles me I keep the volume low and avoid lyrics. Complete silence makes me restless, while loud songs become entertainment. A carefully chosen background track sits at the level where my attention works best.

<!-- word_comment_id: 428; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: With music -->

**Segment 4 — Late at night**

Daytime belongs to everyone else: deliveries, phone calls, traffic, and requests from family. After midnight, the house finally feels like mine. I can solve a problem without someone opening the door halfway through. I tried waking early instead, but my mind remained foggy until breakfast. Late-night study is not ideal before an eight-o'clock class, so I plan it carefully, yet it remains the period when I produce my strongest work.

<!-- word_comment_id: 429; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: Late at night -->

</details>

#### Topic: Studying (2026)

<!-- group_key: listening-p2-set037 -->
<!-- audio_source_key: listening-p2-set037-audio -->
<!-- transcript_status: source_word_comments -->
<!-- word_comment_ids: 430,431,432,433 -->
<details class="audio-transcript">
<summary>Audio transcript</summary>

**Segment 1 — In the coffee shop**

The café owner knows my order before I reach the counter. I choose the table near the back-not beside the noisy coffee machine, but close enough to feel the room's energy. At home, I find chores; in the library, the silence makes me sleepy. The gentle movement of people coming and going somehow keeps me alert. One cup can last two hours, and that small public space has become my most reliable place to study.

<!-- word_comment_id: 430; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: In the coffee shop -->

**Segment 2 — In the park**

My biology notes make more sense outdoors. I sit beneath the large fig tree, spread papers across a picnic blanket, and take a short walk whenever an idea becomes stuck. Children and dogs create some noise, but it is distant and irregular, not the constant conversation of a café. Weather is the obvious limitation. On dry afternoons, though, the park gives me fresh air and enough calm to work for hours.

<!-- word_comment_id: 431; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: In the park -->

**Segment 3 — On public transport**

The train journey to college takes fifty-five minutes, which used to disappear into scrolling on my phone. Now I download readings beforehand and use the same seat table whenever possible. I cannot write neatly while the carriage moves, but flashcards, recorded lectures, and short articles work well. By the time I arrive, one task is already finished. Commuting has become a moving study session rather than dead time.

<!-- word_comment_id: 432; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: On public transport -->

**Segment 4 — At home**

I tried every fashionable study location before admitting the obvious: my own dining table works best. The kettle is nearby, the chair suits my back, and all my books are within reach. I put my phone in another room and ask the family not to interrupt for an hour. Home can contain distractions, but it also gives me control over light, temperature, and breaks. With a little discipline, it is the place where I settle most deeply.

<!-- word_comment_id: 433; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: At home -->

</details>

#### Topic: Restaurant experiences

<!-- group_key: listening-p2-set038 -->
<!-- audio_source_key: listening-p2-set038-audio -->
<!-- transcript_status: source_word_comments -->
<!-- word_comment_ids: 434,435,436,437 -->
<details class="audio-transcript">
<summary>Audio transcript</summary>

**Segment 1 — Was still hungry after dinner**

I'd been meaning to try this restaurant for ages because several people at work had recommended it. The meal itself was actually very good, and I especially liked the dessert. The only problem was the amount of food they served. When I left, I felt as though I'd only had a light snack rather than a full evening meal. I even stopped at a convenience store on the way home to get something else to eat. For the price, I expected a little more.

<!-- word_comment_id: 434; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: Was still hungry after dinner -->

**Segment 2 — Ordered the wrong dish**

My friend had told me that this place was well known for one particular chicken dish, so that was what I planned to have. Unfortunately, the restaurant was very busy and I didn't pay enough attention when the waiter came to our table. It wasn't until my meal arrived that I realised I'd pointed to the item just below the one I actually wanted. I decided not to send it back because it looked good, and in fact I really enjoyed it. Still, I'll have to return if I want to find out why everyone talks about the chicken.

<!-- word_comment_id: 435; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: Ordered the wrong dish -->

**Segment 3 — Met a successful staff member**

We went to a recently opened restaurant near the city centre. While we were waiting for our table, one of the employees started chatting to us. I assumed she was just a regular member of staff, but later one of my friends recognised her photograph on a display near the entrance. Apparently, she had won a national prize for her work in hospitality earlier that year. She was very modest about it and never mentioned the prize herself.

<!-- word_comment_id: 436; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: Met a successful staff member -->

**Segment 4 — Enjoyed a live performance**

At first, I thought the evening would be fairly ordinary. The restaurant was comfortable and the food was good, but nothing really stood out. Then, just as we were finishing the main course, the lights near one end of the room were lowered and a man sat down at the large piano we had noticed earlier. He played for about half an hour, and everyone stopped talking to pay attention. We hadn't expected any entertainment, so it turned out to be the most memorable part of the evening.

<!-- word_comment_id: 437; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: Enjoyed a live performance -->

</details>

#### Topic: Sport

<!-- group_key: listening-p2-set040 -->
<!-- audio_source_key: listening-p2-set040-audio -->
<!-- transcript_status: source_word_comments -->
<!-- word_comment_ids: 442,443,444,445 -->
<details class="audio-transcript">
<summary>Audio transcript</summary>

**Segment 1 — plays in a team sport**

A solo run depends entirely on my own motivation, which is unreliable on cold mornings. Basketball is different. If I miss training, four teammates notice immediately. During a match, every decision connects to someone else: I pass, screen, defend, and trust another player to cover space. Winning feels shared, and even losing gives us something to analyse together. That cooperation is the reason team sport suits me.

<!-- word_comment_id: 442; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: plays in a team sport -->

**Segment 2 — spends time in the water**

On land I am clumsy, but in a pool everything feels smoother. I swim before breakfast three times a week, counting lengths until the lifeguard changes shifts. Summer adds trips to the lake, where I can stay in for hours. Friends invite me to football and running, yet I never enjoy the heat or impact. Give me water, goggles, and a clear lane, and I am completely at home.

<!-- word_comment_id: 443; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: spends time in the water -->

**Segment 3 — plays in the street**

Our neighbourhood had no sports ground, so two drain covers became goalposts and parked cars became the boundaries. We learned to stop the ball whenever a vehicle appeared and continued the match immediately afterwards. I now play at a proper club, but Sunday games still happen outside my parents' house with whoever is available. The street is where I first learned football and where it still feels most joyful.

<!-- word_comment_id: 444; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: plays in the street -->

**Segment 4 — does sports with animals**

Riding is not simply sitting while the horse does the work. My legs, balance, and timing are active every second, and the animal responds to tiny changes I make. Building trust with a nervous horse can take weeks, which makes progress especially rewarding. I enjoy many outdoor sports, but none asks me to cooperate with another living creature in the same way.

<!-- word_comment_id: 445; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: does sports with animals -->

</details>

#### Topic: Reading books (2026)

<!-- group_key: listening-p2-set041 -->
<!-- audio_source_key: listening-p2-set041-audio -->
<!-- transcript_status: source_word_comments -->
<!-- word_comment_ids: 446,447,448,449 -->
<details class="audio-transcript">
<summary>Audio transcript</summary>

**Segment 1 — read when travelling to school**

At home, my phone always wins. On the school bus, however, the signal disappears through two villages, so I open a book instead. The journey lasts about thirty-five minutes-long enough for a chapter, short enough that I rarely become tired of it. I keep a paperback in the front pocket of my bag and leave my textbooks elsewhere. Most of my voluntary reading now happens between my stop and the school gates.

<!-- word_comment_id: 446; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: read when travelling to school -->

**Segment 2 — don't have time to read**

Three unopened novels sit beside my bed, each bought with sincere plans. Then homework runs late, football practice moves to an extra evening, and my younger brother needs help. When I finally lie down, reading two pages feels impossible. Weekends are filled with family errands and friends. I still like books and keep collecting recommendations; what I lack is a free, awake hour in which to enjoy them.

<!-- word_comment_id: 447; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: don't have time to read -->

**Segment 3 — read on their beds**

Reading on transport makes me dizzy, and the living room television interrupts every sentence. My bed solves both problems. I prop up the pillows, use a small lamp, and read until my eyes begin closing. Sometimes that is ten minutes; on holidays it can be two hours. The routine also helps me stop looking at screens before sleep. My bedroom is not the most imaginative location, but it is where books actually get finished.

<!-- word_comment_id: 448; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: read on their beds -->

**Segment 4 — prefer another activity to reading books**

I understand why people love novels, and I enjoy a good story when a teacher assigns one. Free time is different. I would rather meet friends for basketball, repair my bike, or watch a match. Reading asks me to sit still after a whole day at a desk, which feels like more school. Books are not unpleasant; they simply lose whenever I can choose an activity with movement and other people.

<!-- word_comment_id: 449; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: prefer another activity to reading books -->

</details>

#### Topic: Reading books (2026)

<!-- group_key: listening-p2-set042 -->
<!-- audio_source_key: listening-p2-set042-audio -->
<!-- transcript_status: source_word_comments -->
<!-- word_comment_ids: 450,451,452,453 -->
<details class="audio-transcript">
<summary>Audio transcript</summary>

**Segment 1 — read when travelling to school**

I used to read a lot in my bedroom when I was younger, especially before going to sleep. But these days, I don't really read at home anymore because my phone is always next to me. The only time I read properly is on the bus in the morning. My journey to school takes about thirty minutes, so I usually bring a novel with me and read a few pages before class.

<!-- word_comment_id: 450; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: read when travelling to school -->

**Segment 2 — don't have time to read**

I actually enjoy books, and I've got two new ones on my desk right now. Sometimes I even put one beside my bed, thinking I'll read it before I sleep. But most evenings I'm too tired after homework, football practice, and helping my parents. On weekends, I often plan to read, but something else always comes up. So, at the moment, I hardly read at all.

<!-- word_comment_id: 451; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: don't have time to read -->

**Segment 3 — read on their beds**

Some of my friends read on the train or bus, but I can't do that because it makes me feel a bit sick. I also don't like reading in the living room because the TV is usually on. For me, the best place is my bed. I read there almost every night, even if it's only for ten minutes. It helps me relax and forget about school for a while.

<!-- word_comment_id: 452; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: read on their beds -->

**Segment 4 — prefer another activity to reading books**

My parents keep telling me that reading is important, and I agree with them, really. I sometimes read short stories for school, and I can finish them if I have to. But when I have free time, I'd much rather play basketball or watch videos about sports. Books are okay, but they don't excite me as much as doing something active with my friends.

<!-- word_comment_id: 453; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: prefer another activity to reading books -->

<!-- group_key: listening-p2-set043 -->

<!-- transcript_status: source_word_comments -->
<!-- word_comment_ids: 454,455,456,457 -->
<details class="audio-transcript">
<summary>Audio transcript</summary>

**Segment 1 — was still hungry after dinner**

I went to a new Italian restaurant with my friends last Friday. The place looked lovely, and the waiter recommended the chef's special pasta. It tasted good, but the portion was really small. We also shared a salad and some bread, but when I got home, I still had to make myself a sandwich. So, overall, the food was nice, but it definitely wasn't enough for me.

<!-- word_comment_id: 454; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: was still hungry after dinner -->

**Segment 2 — paid a high price**

My family chose a seafood restaurant near the beach for my dad's birthday. The view was amazing, and the dishes were fresh, especially the grilled fish. However, I was shocked when the bill arrived. We didn't order that much, but everything cost more than I expected. The service was fine, but I don't think I'd go back unless it was a very special occasion.

<!-- word_comment_id: 455; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: paid a high price -->

I had dinner at a small Japanese restaurant yesterday. At first, I couldn't understand the menu because many dishes had unfamiliar names. Luckily, one of the staff members came over and explained everything patiently. She even suggested a dish without peanuts because I told her I had an allergy. The food was good, but what I appreciated most was how kind and helpful the staff were.

<!-- word_comment_id: 456; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: met the helpful staff -->

**Segment 4 — watched a perfect performance**

Last weekend, I went to a restaurant that had live music in the evening. The food was ordinary, and honestly, the drinks were a little expensive. But the singer and the band were fantastic. They played beautifully, and everyone in the restaurant stopped talking just to listen. It felt like a mini concert, and I couldn't find a single mistake in their performance.

<!-- word_comment_id: 457; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: watched a perfect performance -->

#### Topic: Shopping Habits (2026)

<!-- group_key: listening-p2-set044 -->
<!-- audio_source_key: listening-p2-set044-audio -->
<!-- transcript_status: source_word_comments -->
<!-- word_comment_ids: 458,459,460,461 -->
<details class="audio-transcript">
<summary>Audio transcript</summary>

**Segment 1 — has limited time for shopping**

I used to enjoy browsing different shops, but work now takes up most of my week. When I need groceries or household items, I prepare a list and choose one place where I can buy everything. I rarely stop to look at displays or compare products for long. If the trip cannot be completed , I normally postpone it or place an order during my lunch break.

<!-- word_comment_id: 458; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: has limited time for shopping -->

**Segment 2 — tries to save money**

I almost never buy an item as soon as I see it. Before deciding, I compare prices across several shops, check delivery charges and search for discount codes. For expensive purchases, I may wait until a seasonal sale begins. Occasionally, the product sells out before I order it, but I would still rather miss a bargain than discover that I paid much more than necessary.

<!-- word_comment_id: 459; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: tries to save money -->

**Segment 3 — considers the environmental impact**

I once chose products mainly because they were cheap or attractive. Now I also notice how much packaging they use and whether the materials can be recycled. When possible, I choose refillable containers, second-hand clothes or goods made locally. I am not perfect and still buy imported products sometimes, but I have become more aware that ordinary shopping choices can affect the world beyond my home.

<!-- word_comment_id: 460; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: considers the environmental impact -->

**Segment 4 — shops only on the internet**

I cannot remember the last time I spent an afternoon inside a shopping centre. I use several apps to order food, clothes and household products, usually while I am at home or travelling. Not being able to examine an item first can be inconvenient, especially with clothing. Even so, I continue to order everything digitally and have it delivered rather than visiting a store myself.

<!-- word_comment_id: 461; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: shops only on the internet -->

</details>
