### **Part 3: Opinion Matching / Discussion - Question 15**

#### Topic: Changes in the workplace

<!-- group_key: listening-p3-set001 -->
<!-- audio_source_key: listening-p3-set001-audio -->
<!-- transcript_status: source_word_comment -->
<!-- word_comment_ids: 462 -->
<details class="audio-transcript">
<summary>Audio transcript</summary>

W: I'm thinking about switching to another job.
M: Why? Your current job is very stable.
W: I think I should try my hand at different fields. The jobs I take should only last around 1-2 years. I don't like staying too long in one job or with one company; that would be really boring. Nowadays, many companies offer temporary jobs, which allows me to try out different fields.
M: Oh, but if you stay longer at one company, they might offer you more opportunities for career advancement. I always want to stay long-term with a company that suits me.
W: Nowadays, young people who have just graduated from university are very talented and have many qualifications. I'm afraid that if I stick with one job and don't change to accumulate knowledge in various fields, it will be hard to compete with these young, talented people.
M: We also need to consider job satisfaction. If we do well, we need to be encouraged and motivated. That helps employees feel that their efforts and contributions to the company are worthwhile.
W: You're absolutely right, I couldn't agree more. In this age of technological advancement, many jobs have been replaced by machines. More and more people are losing their jobs due to the development of technology and engineering. I think this is a concerning issue.
M: I don't think so. Technology is helping us do our work more quickly, easily, and conveniently than ever. If we know how to leverage technology in our jobs, productivity will increase significantly, which in turn helps develop the economy and society.

<!-- word_comment_id: 462; author: Châu Phạm; anchor: Topic: Changes in the workplace -->

</details>

<!-- source_key: listening-p3-set001-q003 -->
| 3. Job satisfaction is important for motivator | B |

#### Topic: Beauty

<!-- group_key: listening-p3-set002 -->
<!-- audio_source_key: listening-p3-set002-audio -->
<!-- transcript_status: source_word_comment -->
<!-- word_comment_ids: 463 -->
<details class="audio-transcript">

W: Hi, what are you reading?
M: I'm reading an article about the concept of beauty.
W: Oh really, that sounds interesting. Beauty is something that can't be defined because everyone has a different perspective on it, right?
M: Actually, people have quite similar perspectives on beauty. We always prefer beautiful things and are drawn to them.
W: Beauty in the past and now is very different. The older generation might see traditional things as beautiful, while the younger generation considers unique and unconventional things as beautiful. So, not everyone has the same view on beauty.
M: Beauty can be found in many places we wouldn't expect, or even within something considered ugly, there's always some beauty present. So, nothing in the world is entirely ugly.
W: Exactly. That's why the definition of beauty is constantly changing. In a few decades, people might find things beautiful that we once considered ugly.
M: That's one perspective. Let's wait and see how it changes.

<!-- word_comment_id: 463; author: Châu Phạm; anchor: Topic: Beauty -->

#### Topic: Actors

<!-- group_key: listening-p3-set003 -->
<!-- audio_source_key: listening-p3-set003-audio -->
<!-- transcript_status: source_word_comment -->
<!-- word_comment_ids: 464 -->
<details class="audio-transcript">
<summary>Audio transcript</summary>

W: What do you think about casting actors before casting them?

M: I think it's absolutely necessary and the most important thing because it determines whether the actor is suitable for the role or not.

W: I understand that auditions are important. But if we only rely on a 15 to 20 minute audition, that short period of time cannot really show us the actor's full potential.

M: That's a good point. So I think it's better to let actors perform freestyle, not relying on the script, so that they can improvise, thereby clearly showing their acting skills.

W: But actors often respond best to a good script. And they also need a script provided by the director and directed to act according to the script. Actors can’t always act freely.

M: What do you think about stage actors moving to film acting?

W: I think they are two completely different fields. When I was studying acting in college, stage acting and film acting were two completely separate subjects. They require different skills and qualities from the actor. So, it’s not easy to move from stage acting to film acting and vice versa.

M: That’s right. These are two very different forms of acting and they require different skills from the actor. However, as actors, I think we all need encouragement and support from the audience.

W: I completely agree. Actors need praise and feedback to feel recognized for their efforts and contributions.

<!-- word_comment_id: 464; author: Châu Phạm; anchor: Topic: Actors -->

</details>

<!-- source_key: listening-p3-set003-q004 -->

#### Topic: The Internet (2026)

<!-- group_key: listening-p3-set005 -->
<!-- audio_source_key: listening-p3-set005-audio -->
<!-- transcript_status: source_word_comment -->
<!-- word_comment_ids: 465 -->
<details class="audio-transcript">
<summary>Audio transcript</summary>

W: You know, I really feel there's too much information on the Internet. It can be overwhelming sometimes. It's hard to know what's credible.
M: Honestly, I think it's more about how we search for information. Finding what we need online requires certain skills. We need to know how to filter through all that content.
W: Definitely! If you don't know how to search properly, you can easily get lost in all that noise.
M: And I believe the Internet affects the way we think. With so much information available, we often skim rather than think deeply about topics.
W: That's an interesting point. I hadn't thought about that. But I do see how the constant scrolling and instant answers can make young people less patient.
M: Exactly! They expect instant results. It's like waiting for anything has become a challenge.
W: Right! So, while the Internet offers us a wealth of knowledge, it can also reduce our patience and tolerance.

<!-- word_comment_id: 465; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: Topic: The Internet (2026) -->

W: Did you hear the callers on the youth council programme last night? Half of them were under twenty-five, and they were making sharper points than some of the older guests.
M: I did. At the community forum last month, the front rows were full of students. Young people clearly see politics as something that affects rent, transport, and education, not as a distant subject.
W: Social media has helped create that change. A short clip from a council meeting can reach thousands of people before the evening news, and campaigns can organise volunteers almost overnight.
M: It changes campaign communication, but I would not say it changes politics itself, and I would not rely on it for understanding. I am more impressed that people now read long-form reports, compare newspapers, and check official data.
W: So you think the public is better informed than before?
M: Overall, yes. There is still misinformation, but people have more tools and many are learning to verify claims.
W: Another encouraging sign is the number of women standing in local elections. Our district used to have one female candidate; this year there are seven.
M: That is a real improvement. Political institutions make better decisions when women are present in greater numbers.
W: And younger voters can see leadership that looks more like the society it represents.

#### Topic: University education (2026)

<!-- group_key: listening-p3-set007 -->
<!-- audio_source_key: listening-p3-set007-audio -->
<!-- transcript_status: source_word_comment -->
<!-- word_comment_ids: 467 -->
<details class="audio-transcript">
<summary>Audio transcript</summary>

W: That looks interesting. What are you up to?
M: Oh, I just came across this interesting report about universities and how they're evolving in the digital age. It talked about the impact of the internet, student experiences, curriculum choices, and even competition between institutions. It got me thinking, there's so much to unpack when it comes to how education is changing, don't you think?
W: Yeah, I've been reading about similar things lately. It's amazing how different opinions can be on what makes a good university experience. I think we can both agree that the internet has made education more accessible. It's great how people from different backgrounds can now access higher education without being physically on campus.
M: Absolutely, I agree with that. It's a game-changer for those who can't afford to relocate or attend in person. But I still believe that social interactions are a crucial part of university life. Online education can never truly replace the friendships, group work, and networking that happen in a physical setting.
W: Hmm, I'm not so sure. While social interactions can be important, I think online learning allows students to focus more on their studies without all the distractions of campus life. Not everyone benefits from that kind of environment.
W: To be honest, I'm not a fan of having too many options in the curriculum. A diverse curriculum sounds good in theory, but it can overwhelm students and make it harder for them to focus on the core subjects they really need.
M: That's one way to look at it, but I think universities provide a unique space for students to develop their social skills alongside their academic ones. Another thing I'm really in favor of is encouraging competition between universities. When universities compete, it drives innovation and pushes them to offer better resources.
W: I disagree there. Competition can sometimes lead to universities focusing too much on rankings and flashy features instead of the of education. It can become more about marketing than genuine improvement.
M: That's a fair concern. But competition also forces universities to constantly improve, which can benefit students in the long run.

<!-- word_comment_id: 467; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: Topic: University education (2026) -->

</details>

W: I find the idea of urban farming intriguing too, but I believe living space is more important than farming space. People need proper homes to thrive.
M: I understand that perspective, but I have to say, farming space in urban areas is really appealing. It brings a bit of nature into the city and can enhance the overall environment.
W: I agree with that. Urban farming does have a lot of visual and ecological benefits. However, we really need to focus on the fact that there's a pressing need for more food in our cities. That should be a priority.
M: That's a valid point, but I think farming space will also benefit the urban economy. It creates jobs and stimulates local businesses, which is essential for growth.
W: I see where you're coming from, but while boosting the economy is great, we can't overlook the immediate need for more food. Urban farming can help, but it shouldn't replace the need for larger-scale solutions to food shortages.
M: I agree that food scarcity is a serious issue, but if we can implement urban farming effectively, it can definitely contribute to alleviating that problem while also strengthening the economy.

#### Topic: Auditions

<!-- group_key: listening-p3-set009 -->
<!-- audio_source_key: listening-p3-set009-audio -->
<!-- transcript_status: source_word_comment -->
<!-- word_comment_ids: 469 -->
<details class="audio-transcript">
<summary>Audio transcript</summary>

M: You know, I've always believed that auditions are the most important part of an actor's career. I mean, that's where the magic happens. It doesn't matter how great you are in rehearsals or even in the final performance; if you can't nail that audition, you're not getting anywhere.
W: Yeah, but it's not just about the audition. I've seen actors who can absolutely shine when they're given the right material to work with. Strong scripts really bring out the best in them. If the writing is weak, even a talented actor might struggle to connect. I think actors respond best when they feel inspired by what's on the page.
M: That's true, but whether it's a strong script or a weak one, they still have to nail that first audition to even get the chance to perform, right?
W: Sure, the audition is key, but once they're in, the script can make or break their performance. It's a balance between both.
M: I agree with that. But I also think there's a huge difference between theatre acting and movie acting. The two require completely different skill sets. In theatre, you have to project and be larger than life, whereas in movies, it's all about subtlety, keeping everything smaller and more contained.
W: Absolutely! They're such different worlds. The way you use your body, your voice, and even how you interact with your fellow actors is completely different depending on whether you're on stage or in front of a camera.
M: Right, and that's why actors deserve as much praise as possible. They're constantly shifting between styles and adapting to new roles. It's tough!
W: Couldn't agree more. Whether it's theatre or film, acting is such an emotional and demanding job. Encouragement and praise go a long way. Actors need to feel appreciated for all the work they put in.

<!-- word_comment_id: 469; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: Topic: Auditions -->

</details>

| Item | Answer / matching detail |
|---|---|
<!-- source_key: listening-p3-set009-q001 -->

<!-- source_key: listening-p3-set009-q002 -->
| 2. Actors tend to respond best to strong and engaging scripts. | W |
<!-- source_key: listening-p3-set009-q003 -->

<!-- source_key: listening-p3-set009-q004 -->

#### Topic: Community Design (2026)

<!-- group_key: listening-p3-set010 -->
<!-- audio_source_key: listening-p3-set010-audio -->
<!-- transcript_status: source_word_comment -->
<!-- word_comment_ids: 470 -->
<details class="audio-transcript">
<summary>Audio transcript</summary>

W: The renovated square felt completely different during yesterday's market. Before the changes, people hurried across it. Now the curved benches and wide paths almost make strangers slow down and speak.
M: I noticed that too. My son started playing near the fountain, and within minutes three families were sharing toys. Design can influence behaviour more than I expected.
W: That is why I support shared courtyards, gardens, and places where people naturally meet.
M: They create opportunities, yes, but they do not create trust overnight. In my apartment building, it took months before neighbours stopped giving each other polite smiles and began offering real help.
W: Community can develop faster in a workplace, though. Teams spend every day solving problems together.
M: Perhaps, but work relationships feel different to me.
W: I do not think the difference is fundamental. Whether people meet through an office, a sports club, or a neighbourhood group, a community is still built on belonging, support, and shared responsibility.
M: I would separate professional and social communities, but technology has certainly blurred those boundaries.
W: Definitely. Our local parenting group began online during the floods, and members still exchange childcare and emergency information.
M: My closest photography group is also online. Some of us have never met, yet we critique each other's work every week.
W: So technology has changed not only where communities meet, but how they begin.

<!-- word_comment_id: 470; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: Topic: Community Design (2026) -->

</details>

#### Topic: Changes in the workplace (2026)

<!-- group_key: listening-p3-set011 -->
<!-- audio_source_key: listening-p3-set011-audio -->
<!-- transcript_status: source_word_comment -->

<details class="audio-transcript">

M: Since the merger, every department seems determined to replace its systems, reporting lines, and even job titles at once. Some continuity is essential, or employees lose the routines that keep the organisation functioning.
W: I understand the need for stability, but people should not confuse routine with security. Three colleagues were promised long-term roles last year and were still made redundant. Job security simply cannot always be guaranteed now.
M: I still think responsible employers can offer reasonable stability. The factor that certainly matters, though, is satisfaction. When people find meaning in the work itself, they are more willing to cope with change.
W: I agree completely. A higher salary may attract someone, but feeling respected and useful is often what keeps them.
M: Our design team stayed through the merger because they still believed in the product.
W: Whereas the sales team left after managers stopped listening to them.
M: Technology may at least make the transition worthwhile. Better automation can reduce waste, help small firms compete, and raise productivity across many industries.
W: It can also remove roles and increase pressure, so I would not call every technological change positive.
M: Not every individual change, no. But for the wider economy, innovation creates new services and opportunities that old systems cannot provide.
W: Provided organisations help workers adapt rather than treating them as disposable.

W: I believe that the younger generation and future generations will become increasingly smarter, and they'll discover even more amazing things about information technology to benefit society.

#### Topic: Singers and music (2026)

<!-- group_key: listening-p3-set013 -->

<!-- transcript_status: source_word_comment -->
<!-- word_comment_ids: 473 -->
<details class="audio-transcript">
<summary>Audio transcript</summary>

W: The teenagers in tonight's choir kept talking about the singer who funded their music room. That is why I think singers can be good role models. Their choices can encourage young fans to practise, volunteer, or speak confidently.
M: Some can, though fame alone does not make anyone worth copying.
W: Of course not. I mean artists who use their influence responsibly, not every celebrity with a microphone.
M: Musical taste is too personal for universal heroes anyway. My favourite singer sounds powerful to me and unbearable to my brother.
W: Exactly. Our preferences come from memory, culture, age, and personality. There is no objective playlist that everyone should love.
M: Yet I still think music itself is a universal language. At an international festival, people may not share vocabulary, but rhythm and melody allow them to respond together.
W: I am less certain. Cultural meanings can be very different, even if people enjoy the same beat.
M: Films rely on that shared emotional response all the time.
W: They also show how music can manipulate feelings. A harmless character seems threatening when low strings begin.
M: And an ordinary product can feel exciting in an advertisement because of the soundtrack.
W: So on that point we agree. Music does not merely accompany emotion; it can actively steer it.

<!-- word_comment_id: 473; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: Topic: Singers and music (2026) -->

#### Topic: Beauty (2026)

<!-- group_key: listening-p3-set014 -->
<!-- audio_source_key: listening-p3-set014-audio -->
<!-- transcript_status: source_word_comment -->
<!-- word_comment_ids: 474 -->
<details class="audio-transcript">

M: Look at these portraits from the 1990s. Different photographers, yet the faces, poses, and body shapes are remarkably similar. People often share the same idea of what beauty should look like.
W: I think that similarity was imposed by magazines rather than genuinely shared by everyone, and the next room proves that ideas change over time. The eyebrows, clothing, and hairstyles praised thirty years ago are already returning after being mocked for years.
M: The repeated styles make me think beauty ideals are more stable than they appear. Still, the most beautiful photograph here is not a portrait at all; it is that cracked bus shelter reflecting the sunrise.
W: I love that one. Beauty often appears in unlikely places, including rust on a wall, a tired person's laugh, and shadows across an empty factory.
M: Perhaps photographers train us to notice what everyone else walks past.
W: And society is beginning to notice different kinds of people too. Traditional beauty standards are going to change as unedited images and more varied faces become visible.
M: Or the internet may simply create a new uniform standard.
W: It might, but younger audiences are challenging old rules about age, skin, body size, and gender.
M: I hope they keep the freedom to disagree about beauty.
W: That disagreement is evidence that the definition is still evolving.

<!-- word_comment_id: 474; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: Topic: Beauty (2026) -->

#### Topic: The arts (2026)

<!-- group_key: listening-p3-set015 -->

W: The gallery ticket costs more than my weekly food budget. People say art belongs to everyone, but in practice it is suitable mainly for the privileged few who have money and free time.
M: I would not say art is only for privileged people; street art, libraries, and free performances already exist. Even so, public investment matters. The government should fund museums, libraries, street performances, and community workshops instead of leaving culture entirely to the market.
W: I am not convinced government funding is the main answer. Schools matter even earlier. My first visit to a theatre at age nine changed what I thought I could do.
M: Same for me. Children should encounter painting, music, dance, and drama before they decide that art is not for them.
W: Not only the children whose parents can pay for lessons.
M: Agreed. And I think access will expand in the future. Digital archives, low-cost creative software, and virtual exhibitions already let people see work that used to be hidden in private collections.
W: Online access is convenient, but I do not think it will make art genuinely accessible by itself. It cannot replace standing in front of an original painting.
M: No, but it can be the first doorway. Someone who discovers an artist on a phone may later visit a gallery or join a class.
W: Provided that doorway is not locked behind another subscription.
M: That brings us back to public investment and genuine access.

#### Topic: Children and young people with technology (2026)

<!-- group_key: listening-p3-set016 -->

<!-- transcript_status: source_word_comment -->

M: I've been thinking about how children and teenagers use technology these days. Many of them seem really interested in learning through computers, tablets, and educational apps. Digital tools can make lessons feel more active and enjoyable.
W: I agree. Students often become more motivated when technology is used in class. Interactive games, videos, and online exercises can make learning more exciting for them.
M: However, I do worry that too much technology can affect their communication skills. Some children are comfortable sending messages online, but they find it harder to speak to people face to face.
W: Yes, I've noticed that too. Some young people can write online, but when they have to talk directly to classmates or adults, they may feel nervous or unsure.
W: Another problem is that many families don't really know how to manage children's technology use at home. Parents may not know how long children should spend online or what kinds of websites are suitable. I think families need more guidance.
M: I'm not sure families always need outside help. I think children themselves can learn to control their online behaviour if they are taught properly. With the right education, they can learn when to stop using screens and how to communicate responsibly online. **Explanation summary** *Both speakers say children are interested in learning through technology and can struggle with face-to-face communication. #### Topic: A new environmental volunteer program (2026)

<!-- group_key: listening-p3-set017 -->
<!-- audio_source_key: listening-p3-set017-audio -->
<!-- transcript_status: source_word_comment -->
<!-- word_comment_ids: 477 -->
<details class="audio-transcript">

M: I've read several articles about the new environmental volunteer program, and I think the media has exaggerated its benefits. The project sounds useful, but it won't completely change the city in just a few weeks.
W: I agree. The media often makes these programs sound bigger than they really are. Volunteers can help clean parks and streets, but one program cannot solve every environmental problem.
W: In fact, I think littering will always be a problem. Some people simply don't care about public spaces, no matter how many volunteers clean them. That's why I believe punishment is the most effective solution. If people have to pay fines, they will think twice before throwing rubbish on the ground.
M: I'm not sure punishment is the best answer. Education and community activities can also change people's habits over time. And I don't think volunteers' personal motivations affect the outcome of the program. Some may join because they care about the environment, while others may want experience for their resume, but if they collect rubbish and improve public spaces, the result is still positive.
W: Maybe, but I still think fines are necessary. Without strict punishment, many people will continue littering because they know there are no serious consequences. **Explanation summary** *Both speakers say the media exaggerates the benefits of the program. The woman says littering will always be a problem and believes punishment is the most effective solution. The man says volunteers' motivations do not affect the useful outcome of the program.*

#### Topic: The Internet (Version 2) (2026)

<!-- group_key: listening-p3-set018 -->
<!-- audio_source_key: listening-p3-set018-audio -->
<!-- transcript_status: source_word_comment -->
<!-- word_comment_ids: 478 -->
<details class="audio-transcript">
<summary>Audio transcript</summary>

W: These days, I feel there is too much information on the internet. Sometimes when I look for something online, I don't know which result is useful or reliable.
M: I completely agree. There is so much information available that it can become confusing. A simple search can give thousands of results, and many of them are not very helpful.
M: That's why I think using the internet properly requires real skills. People need to know how to search carefully, compare sources, and check whether information is trustworthy.
W: That's an interesting point. I suppose many people don't think carefully enough about how they search online.
M: The internet is also changing the way we think. Because we see so many opinions and answers, we often compare information instead of thinking deeply.
W: Yes, I agree. People don't accept information in the same way as before. They read different views online and then decide what they believe.
M: Another issue is that the internet has made people less patient. We expect answers immediately because everything appears so online.
W: Exactly. When people are used to instant results, waiting for anything can feel difficult. **Explanation summary** *Both speakers say there is too much information online. The man specifically says using the internet requires search and evaluation skills. Both agree that the internet changes how people think and makes people less patient.*

<!-- word_comment_id: 478; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: Topic: The Internet (Version 2) (2026) -->

</details>

#### Topic: Local Cultural Differences (2026)

<!-- group_key: listening-p3-set019 -->
<!-- audio_source_key: listening-p3-set019-audio -->
<!-- transcript_status: source_word_comment -->
<!-- word_comment_ids: 479 -->
<details class="audio-transcript">
<summary>Audio transcript</summary>

M: Have you ever noticed how different local cultures can be, even within the same country?
W: Yes, definitely. Food, festivals, customs, and even everyday behaviour can change a lot from one region to another. In my opinion, language is the biggest difficulty when people try to understand a local culture. Regional dialects and accents can make communication really confusing.
M: I see what you mean, but I don't think language is always the biggest problem. Sometimes values, traditions, and social rules are even harder to understand.
W: That's true. I'm also worried that globalisation is changing local traditions. International trends are becoming more popular, and some traditional customs are slowly disappearing.
M: I agree with that. Globalisation is definitely changing people's lifestyles. Local traditions are not as strong as they used to be in many places.
M: What concerns me most is that the younger generation does not seem to care as much about traditional culture. Many young people are more interested in global music, fashion, and online trends than in their own local heritage.
W: I'm not completely sure about that. Some young people still feel proud of their roots and take part in cultural festivals or family traditions.
M: Maybe, but I still think visiting places in person is the most effective way to learn about different cultures. When you travel somewhere, talk to local people, try the food, and see the traditions yourself, you understand much more than by only reading about it.
W: Travelling is helpful, but I think online materials, books, and cultural events can also teach people a lot. **Explanation summary** *The woman says language is the biggest difficulty in understanding local culture. Both speakers agree globalisation is changing local traditions. The man says young people care less about traditional culture and that visiting places in person is the most effective way to learn about cultural differences.*

<!-- word_comment_id: 479; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: Topic: Local Cultural Differences (2026) -->

</details>

| Item | Answer / matching detail |
|---|---|
<!-- source_key: listening-p3-set019-q001 -->
| 1. Language is the biggest difficulty when people try to understand a local culture | W |
<!-- source_key: listening-p3-set019-q002 -->
| 2. Globalisation is changing local traditions and ways of life | B |
<!-- source_key: listening-p3-set019-q003 -->
| 3. The younger generation does not care as much about their traditional culture | M |
<!-- source_key: listening-p3-set019-q004 -->
| 4. Visiting places in person is the most effective way to learn about different cultures | M |

#### Topic: Urban farming (Version 2) (2026)

<!-- group_key: listening-p3-set020 -->
<!-- audio_source_key: listening-p3-set020-audio -->
<!-- transcript_status: source_word_comment -->
<!-- word_comment_ids: 480 -->
<details class="audio-transcript">
<summary>Audio transcript</summary>

W: Hey Carlos, have you read the article about urban farming?
M: Urban farming? What exactly is it?
W: Well, it's about growing food in urban areas, like on rooftops or unused spaces to help address food supply issues in cities.
M: You mean farming in cities? How could that even work? Cities are already overcrowded. There isn't enough space for housing, let alone farming. Urban farming will take up land that could be used for accommodation.
W: I think it might work. More people from rural areas are moving to cities, and food has become a critical issue. It's vital that we adapt over time.
M: That's exactly my point. Where are all these people supposed to live?
W: You don't have to take urban farming literally. It's not about large-scale farming right away. Small changes are necessary. Over time, we need to find ways to balance ecological and economic needs.
M: Sure, I support green initiatives, but they need to be practical. How can this approach actually work?
W: Unused spaces that are too small for housing can be converted into farming areas. If done right, this could be both beneficial and visually attractive, like turning them into green spaces or local parks.
M: I still think it could have negative effects, especially on rural areas where traditional farmers might lose their jobs if urban farming takes over.
W: You should think of it as a supplement, not a replacement. For rural areas, producing vegetables closer to where they're consumed can actually be beneficial. It saves on transportation costs and uses resources more efficiently.
M: But this is mostly about food, right? I don't think urban farming can solve the problem of scarcity. Cities just can't realistically support large-scale farming. Rural farming is still a more practical option.
W: That's true, and I agree. But changes need to happen. Sooner or later, urban farming will become a key trend for the future. **Explanation summary** *The man argues that housing space is more important because cities are overcrowded and urban farming could take land for accommodation. The woman supports urban farming as useful and attractive, says it can save transportation costs and resources, and both speakers discuss food scarcity as a real issue.*

<!-- word_comment_id: 480; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: Topic: Urban farming (Version 2) (2026) -->

</details>

| Item | Answer / matching detail |
|---|---|
<!-- source_key: listening-p3-set020-q001 -->
| 1. Housing space is more important than farming space. | M |
<!-- source_key: listening-p3-set020-q002 -->
| 2. Urban farming is appealing. | W |
<!-- source_key: listening-p3-set020-q003 -->
| 3. Urban farming will benefit the urban economy. | W |
<!-- source_key: listening-p3-set020-q004 -->
| 4. Cities need more food. | B |

#### Topic: Actors (Version 3) (2026)

<!-- group_key: listening-p3-set021 -->
<!-- audio_source_key: listening-p3-set021-audio -->
<!-- transcript_status: source_word_comment -->
<!-- word_comment_ids: 481 -->
<details class="audio-transcript">
<summary>Audio transcript</summary>

M: The festival audience applauded the final performance, but the director told us the actor nearly lost the role at the first audition. That proves how important auditions are; they can determine an entire career in ten minutes.
W: Or it proves how unreliable they are. She became convincing only after working with the screenplay, and strong, engaging writing gave her emotional detail that the audition extract lacked.
M: Still, without a memorable audition she would never have entered rehearsal.
W: I accept that it opens the door, but the script shapes what happens after the door opens.
M: The panel also discussed her move from theatre to film. She had to reduce every gesture because screen acting and stage acting are genuinely different crafts.
W: Absolutely. On stage, breath and movement must reach hundreds of people; on film, the camera can detect tension in a fingertip.
M: That versatility deserves more recognition. Actors are expected to transform physically, learn accents, handle rejection, and remain emotionally available.
W: I agree. Audiences often praise the director and forget the performer who repeats a painful scene twelve times.
M: Good acting should be recognised, not treated as effortless.
W: And praise should include disciplined supporting actors, not only stars. **Explanation summary** *User-provided Part 3 topic version for Actors ( version 3 ). Check the statement-level explanations for the final speaker mapping.*

<!-- word_comment_id: 481; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: Topic: Actors (Version 3) (2026) -->

</details>

| Item | Answer / matching detail |
|---|---|
<!-- source_key: listening-p3-set021-q001 -->
| 1. Auditions are most important for an actor's career. | M |
<!-- source_key: listening-p3-set021-q002 -->
| 2. Actors tend to respond best to strong and engaging scripts. | W |
<!-- source_key: listening-p3-set021-q003 -->
| 3. Theater acting and screen acting are quite different from each other. | B |
<!-- source_key: listening-p3-set021-q004 -->
| 4. Actors should be praised and recognized as much as possible. | B |

#### Topic: Actors (Version 1) (2026)

<!-- group_key: listening-p3-set022 -->
<!-- audio_source_key: listening-p3-set022-audio -->
<!-- transcript_status: source_word_comment -->
<!-- word_comment_ids: 482 -->
<details class="audio-transcript">
<summary>Audio transcript</summary>

W: The school cast looked terrified before today's try-outs. Do you really believe an audition is the most important part of an actor's career?
M: For professional work, yes. It may be unfair, but the audition is where an actor first convinces someone to take a risk on them. Without that opportunity, later talent stays invisible.
W: I watched one student freeze during the monologue and then become excellent when we gave her the full scene. Actors often respond best to strong scripts because context gives them something real to play.
M: A good script helps, though they still need technique.
W: Of course. We should also teach them that theatre acting and screen acting are not interchangeable.
M: Definitely. In the hall, they need a clear voice and larger movement. For the video project, the microphone and close-up allow much smaller choices.
W: One boy kept projecting to the back wall while the camera was centimetres from his face.
M: Learning both styles is demanding, which is why actors deserve more praise and recognition.
W: I agree completely. They are judged publicly while doing emotionally difficult work.
M: Even these students deserve encouragement after facing a panel.
W: Then let us give notes carefully and praise the risks they took. **Explanation summary** *User-provided Part 3 topic version for Actors ( version 1 ). Check the statement-level explanations for the final speaker mapping.*

<!-- word_comment_id: 482; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: Topic: Actors (Version 1) (2026) -->

</details>

| Item | Answer / matching detail |
|---|---|
<!-- source_key: listening-p3-set022-q001 -->

| 2. Actors tend to respond best to strong and engaging scripts. | W |
<!-- source_key: listening-p3-set022-q003 -->

#### Topic: Politics (Version 3) (2026)

<!-- group_key: listening-p3-set023 -->
<!-- audio_source_key: listening-p3-set023-audio -->
<!-- transcript_status: source_word_comment -->
<!-- word_comment_ids: 483 -->
<details class="audio-transcript">
<summary>Audio transcript</summary>

W: The student debate was packed last night. A few years ago we could hardly fill one classroom; now young people line up to discuss housing, climate policy, and voting rights.
M: I noticed the same change in my seminars. Politics has become personal to students because they connect public decisions with their own futures.
W: Live streams have helped. Social media changes politics by allowing a small campus campaign to reach people far beyond the university.
M: It also rewards dramatic slogans. I think people are better informed today mainly because more of them read evidence, use fact-checking services, and compare serious sources.
W: Those habits are important, but social platforms can still reveal issues traditional media ignores.
M: They can reveal them without explaining them.
W: Fair criticism. At least representation is improving. Three of the four debate teams were led by women.
M: And more women are pursuing political careers nationally too. That gives younger students examples they did not have before.
W: It also changes which experiences enter public discussion.
M: Agreed. Greater participation from women is not a temporary trend; it strengthens politics. **Explanation summary** *User-provided Part 3 topic version for Politics ( version 3 ). Check the statement-level explanations for the final speaker mapping.*

<!-- word_comment_id: 483; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: Topic: Politics (Version 3) (2026) -->

</details>

|---|---|
<!-- source_key: listening-p3-set023-q001 -->
| 1. Young people are more into politics. | B |
<!-- source_key: listening-p3-set023-q002 -->
| 2. Social media changes politics. | W |
<!-- source_key: listening-p3-set023-q003 -->
| 3. People are now better informed about politics. | M |
<!-- source_key: listening-p3-set023-q004 -->
| 4. More women pursue politics. | B |

#### Topic: Politics (Version 2) (2026)

<!-- group_key: listening-p3-set024 -->
<!-- audio_source_key: listening-p3-set024-audio -->
<!-- transcript_status: source_word_comment -->
<!-- word_comment_ids: 484 -->
<details class="audio-transcript">
<summary>Audio transcript</summary>

W: My niece spent the weekend canvassing for a local candidate. At her age I avoided every political conversation, but young people now seem genuinely interested.
M: My son is the same. He asked us to discuss the transport budget over dinner, which has never happened in this family.
W: Much of that interest begins on social media. Candidates respond to voters in short videos, volunteers organise through group chats, and political stories spread before breakfast.
M: Campaigning has always adapted to new media, so I would not say social media changes politics itself. I think people are now better informed because good podcasts, newsletters, and public databases explain issues in greater depth.
W: Provided they choose reliable ones.
M: Yes. Better access does not automatically mean better understanding.
W: Another difference from our youth is the visibility of women in politics. My niece follows several female mayors and says leadership no longer looks like one type of person.
M: That is encouraging. More women are entering politics, and parties are finally treating that as normal rather than exceptional.
W: There is still a long way to go.
M: Of course, but the direction is clear.
W: And young voters are helping push it forward. **Explanation summary** *User-provided Part 3 topic version for Politics ( version 2 ). Check the statement-level explanations for the final speaker mapping.*

<!-- word_comment_id: 484; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: Topic: Politics (Version 2) (2026) -->

M: The crowd outside the campaign hall was unexpectedly young. Students were interviewing candidates and registering first-time voters.
W: Yes, political interest among young people is much stronger than it was when we were at school.
M: Social media has changed politics enormously. The rally filled up because one volunteer's video was shared thousands of times, and the candidate answered criticism online before the newspaper even printed it.
W: I do not think that changes politics itself; it mainly changes publicity, and it can flatten complicated policies into slogans.
M: True, though it gives ordinary supporters a way to organise.
W: I am more impressed by how well informed many voters have become. During the public session, people cited budgets, compared earlier promises, and challenged misleading statistics.
M: They had clearly done their homework.
W: Another welcome change is the number of women running for office.
M: Absolutely. More women are pursuing politics, and that broadens both leadership styles and policy priorities.
W: The panel tonight was evenly balanced for the first time.
M: I hope that soon nobody considers that remarkable. **Explanation summary** *User-provided Part 3 topic version for Politics ( version 1 ). Check the statement-level explanations for the final speaker mapping.*

#### Topic: Urban Farming (Version 4) (2026)

<!-- group_key: listening-p3-set026 -->
<!-- audio_source_key: listening-p3-set026-audio -->
<!-- transcript_status: source_word_comment -->
<!-- word_comment_ids: 486 -->
<details class="audio-transcript">
<summary>Audio transcript</summary>

W: Our building committee wants a greenhouse on the roof, and the architect has brought three designs for tonight's meeting.
M: Before we admire drawings, remember that the city lacks affordable homes. Housing space is more important than farming space, even if rooftop gardens sound fashionable.
W: This roof cannot legally support apartments, but it can support lightweight growing beds. The best design includes trees, seating, and a small market, so it would make the block far more attractive.
M: I doubt a greenhouse would make the building more attractive for long; poorly maintained beds could look worse than the empty roof.
W: The cooperative would sell herbs to cafés and employ two residents. It could help the local economy as well as reduce food transport.
M: Two jobs are not an economic revolution.
W: No, and the greenhouse will not solve food shortages by itself.
M: On that we agree. A roof cannot feed an entire district.
W: It can still demonstrate one practical use for wasted space.
M: As long as nobody presents it as a substitute for housing or rural agriculture.
W: Then the proposal should describe it as a small local supplement, not a miracle cure. **Explanation summary** *User-provided Part 3 topic version for Urban Farming ( version 4 ). Check the statement-level explanations for the final speaker mapping.*

<!-- word_comment_id: 486; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: Topic: Urban Farming (Version 4) (2026) -->

</details>

| Item | Answer / matching detail |
|---|---|
<!-- source_key: listening-p3-set026-q001 -->
| 1. Housing space is more important than farming space. | M |
<!-- source_key: listening-p3-set026-q002 -->
| 2. Urban farming can make cities more attractive. | W |
<!-- source_key: listening-p3-set026-q003 -->
| 3. Urban farming could help the local economy. | W |
<!-- source_key: listening-p3-set026-q004 -->
| 4. Urban farming cannot solve food shortages by itself. | B |

#### Topic: Urban Farming (Version 3) (2026)

<!-- group_key: listening-p3-set027 -->
<!-- audio_source_key: listening-p3-set027-audio -->
<!-- transcript_status: source_word_comment -->
<!-- word_comment_ids: 487 -->
<details class="audio-transcript">
<summary>Audio transcript</summary>

M: The documentary crew wants to film an urban farm on the abandoned railway yard. From the bridge, the rows of vegetables already make the area look less neglected.
W: It is pleasant, but I still believe housing space matters more. The waiting list for homes is growing, and the yard is one of the few large sites left.
M: Only part of it is suitable for building. The contaminated section can support raised beds, public paths, and trees.
W: In that case, I agree the farm could make the district more attractive. It would replace fences and rubbish with a space people can actually use.
M: It may also help the local economy. The project plans to train residents, supply street markets, and rent stalls to small food businesses.
W: I am not convinced those jobs and stalls would last, and nobody should claim the project can meet the city's food demand.
M: Certainly not. Urban farming cannot solve shortages by itself.
W: We agree there. It should complement rural producers and supermarkets, not replace them.
M: That distinction would make a good ending for the documentary.
W: Especially if the film also shows the housing debate rather than presenting a perfect green solution. **Explanation summary** *User-provided Part 3 topic version for Urban Farming ( version 3 ). Check the statement-level explanations for the final speaker mapping.*

#### Topic: Urban Farming (Version 1) (2026)

<!-- group_key: listening-p3-set028 -->
<!-- audio_source_key: listening-p3-set028-audio -->
<!-- transcript_status: source_word_comment -->

<details class="audio-transcript">

M: After last year's flood, the multistorey car park has remained half empty. The mayor now wants to turn the top levels into growing spaces.
W: I would rather see the whole structure replaced with housing. People need secure homes more urgently than tomatoes grown above a car park.
M: Demolition would take years, while lightweight beds could be installed within weeks. The proposal also includes shaded walkways and a café overlooking the river.
W: I visited a similar project abroad, and I admit it made an unattractive building feel welcoming. If the public can enter freely, it could improve the area's appearance.
M: The organisers also plan a weekend market. Local food businesses could buy directly, and residents could earn money from training and sales.
W: I doubt a weekend market would create a meaningful economic benefit, and the numbers should be checked carefully.
M: Of course. It will not solve food shortages by itself.
W: No urban project could. The growing area is too small, and cities still depend on rural farms.
M: Then perhaps it is worth doing for several modest benefits rather than one impossible promise.
W: Possibly, but housing must remain the first priority in any future redevelopment. **Explanation summary** *User-provided Part 3 topic version for Urban Farming ( version 1 ). Check the statement-level explanations for the final speaker mapping.*

#### Topic: Environmental Volunteer Program (Version 1) (2026)

<!-- group_key: listening-p3-set029 -->
<!-- audio_source_key: listening-p3-set029-audio -->
<!-- transcript_status: source_word_comment -->
<!-- word_comment_ids: 489 -->
<details class="audio-transcript">
<summary>Audio transcript</summary>

M: The newspaper headline called the volunteer clean-up "a new beginning for the entire city." I was at the canal that day, and the media definitely exaggerated what one small programme achieved.
W: I helped with the photography, and I agree. The article showed the cleanest section and cropped out the rubbish still floating beyond the bridge.
M: That is why I think littering will always be a problem. Some people leave cups beside an empty bin because they assume somebody else will deal with them.
W: I am less pessimistic. Public habits can improve if facilities and education improve.
M: Perhaps, but good intentions are not enough.
W: The volunteers did useful work regardless of their intentions. Some wanted references, some wanted publicity, and some cared deeply about the environment. Their motivations did not affect the final number of bags collected.
M: They may affect whether the programme survives.
W: Yes, but not the outcome of that particular day.
M: To reduce the problem permanently, punishment is the most effective approach. A serious fine changes behaviour faster than another friendly slogan.
W: Fines can help, but they may simply move littering to places with fewer officers.
M: Then enforcement must be consistent.
W: And education must continue beside it. **Explanation summary** *User-provided Part 3 topic version for Environmental Volunteer Program ( version 1 ). Check the statement-level explanations for the final speaker mapping.*

<!-- word_comment_id: 489; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: Topic: Environmental Volunteer Program (Version 1) (2026) -->

</details>

| Item | Answer / matching detail |
|---|---|
<!-- source_key: listening-p3-set029-q001 -->
| 1. Media exaggerates the benefits of the programme. | B |
<!-- source_key: listening-p3-set029-q002 -->
| 2. Littering will always be a problem. | M |
<!-- source_key: listening-p3-set029-q003 -->
| 3. The volunteers' motivations do not affect the programme outcome. | W |
<!-- source_key: listening-p3-set029-q004 -->
| 4. Punishment is the most effective way to prevent littering. | M |

#### Topic: Beauty (Version 2) (2026)

<!-- group_key: listening-p3-set030 -->
<!-- audio_source_key: listening-p3-set030-audio -->
<!-- transcript_status: source_word_comment -->
<!-- word_comment_ids: 490 -->
<details class="audio-transcript">
<summary>Audio transcript</summary>

W: The advertising team rejected three faces because they did not match the "clean beauty" reference board. They call that look universal, but I think it is a corporate template rather than something people genuinely agree on.
M: That suggests people share surprisingly similar ideas about beauty, especially when brands repeat the same face until it feels normal.
W: But those ideas are temporary. Look at old campaigns. Features once hidden are now highlighted, while yesterday's fashionable look already feels dated. Beauty standards change over time.
M: I am not convinced standards truly change; advertising may simply rotate one narrow rule into another.
W: During the location shoot, my favourite image was not a model at all. It was an elderly cleaner arranging bright bottles beside a broken window.
M: I saw that photograph. Beauty can appear in unlikely places, especially when nobody is performing for the camera.
W: Exactly. I think traditional ideas of beauty are going to change further as audiences demand age, disability, texture, and ordinary bodies in public images.
M: Brands may follow only because difference now sells.
W: Commercial motives do not cancel the effect. Repeated images can widen what people accept.
M: As long as a new trend does not become another rigid formula.
W: That is why the goal should be variety, not a replacement standard. **Explanation summary** *User-provided Part 3 topic version for Beauty ( version 2 ). Check the statement-level explanations for the final speaker mapping.*

<!-- word_comment_id: 490; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: Topic: Beauty (Version 2) (2026) -->

</details>

| Item | Answer / matching detail |
|---|---|
<!-- source_key: listening-p3-set030-q001 -->
| 1. People share similar ideas about beauty. | M |
<!-- source_key: listening-p3-set030-q002 -->
| 2. Ideas about beauty change over time. | W |
<!-- source_key: listening-p3-set030-q003 -->
| 3. Beauty can be found in unlikely places. | B |
<!-- source_key: listening-p3-set030-q004 -->
| 4. Traditional ideas about beauty are going to change. | W |

#### Topic: Beauty (Version 1) (2026)

<!-- group_key: listening-p3-set031 -->
<!-- audio_source_key: listening-p3-set031-audio -->
<!-- transcript_status: source_word_comment -->
<!-- word_comment_ids: 491 -->
<details class="audio-transcript">
<summary>Audio transcript</summary>

W: This fashion archive is full of surprises. The guide called a tiny waist timeless, but the next display celebrates broad shoulders, and the one after that praises a completely different shape.
M: Those apparent changes are superficial. People within each period still share similar ideas about beauty. Portrait painters, magazines, and clothing makers all repeat the same ideal until society recognises it.
W: For a while. Then the ideal changes. Beauty standards move with economics, technology, and social values, not with some permanent rule.
M: The most beautiful object here might be that repaired work coat. It was never designed to be elegant.
W: I agree. The faded stitching and handwritten name make it more moving than the formal dresses. Beauty can be found in unlikely places because history gives ordinary things meaning.
M: Museums are good at changing how we look.
W: Society is changing too. Traditional ideas about beauty are going to become less powerful as people see more ages, cultures, and identities represented.
M: I doubt traditional ideas will lose much influence; humans will probably continue forming the same shared preferences.
W: Shared preferences are fine. The problem begins when one preference is treated as a rule for everybody. **Explanation summary** *User-provided Part 3 topic version for Beauty ( version 1 ). Check the statement-level explanations for the final speaker mapping.*

<!-- word_comment_id: 491; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: Topic: Beauty (Version 1) (2026) -->

</details>

| Item | Answer / matching detail |
|---|---|
<!-- source_key: listening-p3-set031-q001 -->
| 1. People share similar ideas about beauty. | M |
<!-- source_key: listening-p3-set031-q002 -->
| 2. Ideas about beauty change over time. | W |
<!-- source_key: listening-p3-set031-q003 -->
| 3. Beauty can be found in unlikely places. | B |
<!-- source_key: listening-p3-set031-q004 -->
| 4. Traditional ideas about beauty are going to change. | W |

#### Topic: University And Technology (Version 3) (2026)

<!-- group_key: listening-p3-set032 -->
<!-- audio_source_key: listening-p3-set032-audio -->
<!-- transcript_status: source_word_comment -->
<!-- word_comment_ids: 492 -->
<details class="audio-transcript">
<summary>Audio transcript</summary>

W: The university wants us to design a degree that students can complete from three countries without moving. That would have been impossible when we studied.
M: It is a strong example of how the internet makes education more accessible. Students with jobs, disabilities, or family responsibilities can join without relocating.
W: And international students can attend guest lectures that were once limited to one campus.
M: Access is valuable, but social interaction remains essential to university life. Students need disagreement, teamwork, informal conversation, and professional networks, not only recorded lectures.
W: Some online groups form real connections, though the course design must deliberately create them.
M: Exactly; they should not be treated as an optional extra.
W: I am more concerned about the proposed list of forty elective modules. A diverse curriculum is not always beneficial. Too many unrelated choices can prevent students from developing depth.
M: Advisers can guide them. I would rather offer variety than force everyone through one path.
W: How do you feel about the dean's plan to compare our results publicly with other universities?
M: I support it. Competition between universities should be encouraged because it pushes institutions to improve teaching and student services.
W: Unless they start teaching to the ranking.
M: That risk needs regulation, not the removal of competition. **Explanation summary** *User-provided Part 3 topic version for University And Technology ( version 3 ). Check the statement-level explanations for the final speaker mapping.*

<!-- word_comment_id: 492; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: Topic: University And Technology (Version 3) (2026) -->

<!-- source_key: listening-p3-set032-q003 -->

<!-- source_key: listening-p3-set032-q004 -->

#### Topic: University And Technology (Version 1) (2026)

<!-- group_key: listening-p3-set033 -->
<!-- audio_source_key: listening-p3-set033-audio -->
<!-- transcript_status: source_word_comment -->
<!-- word_comment_ids: 493 -->
<details class="audio-transcript">
<summary>Audio transcript</summary>

M: These university brochures all promise online lectures, international modules, and flexible study. At least the internet has genuinely made education more accessible.
W: I agree. My cousin can study from her island and keep caring for her father instead of moving to the capital.
M: The brochure treats campus life as a luxury, though.
W: I would call social interaction essential. Students learn to negotiate, cooperate, and form friendships through daily contact. A university experience made entirely of screens would feel incomplete.
M: Online communities can provide some of that, but perhaps not as naturally.
W: Exactly.
M: I dislike the huge curriculum offered here. A diverse curriculum is not always a good thing; students can collect fashionable modules without building a coherent foundation.
W: That depends on academic advising. I am interested in the competition between these universities. Each one has improved laboratories and support because it wants to attract applicants.
M: Competition can encourage wasteful advertising.
W: It can, but it also prevents institutions from assuming students have no alternative. I think competition should be encouraged, with transparent standards.
M: So we agree on access, but not on how much choice and competition education needs.
W: That is probably the most realistic conclusion. **Explanation summary** *User-provided Part 3 topic version for University And Technology ( version 1 ). Check the statement-level explanations for the final speaker mapping.*

#### Topic: The Arts (Version 2) (2026)

<!-- group_key: listening-p3-set034 -->
<!-- audio_source_key: listening-p3-set034-audio -->
<!-- transcript_status: source_word_comment -->
<!-- word_comment_ids: 494 -->
<details class="audio-transcript">
<summary>Audio transcript</summary>

M: The new private gallery charges so much that ordinary families will never enter. In situations like this, art really is suitable only for a privileged few.
W: That gallery is only one example, so I would not say art itself belongs only to the privileged. Still, public culture should not depend on wealthy collectors. The government ought to invest more in arts centres, festivals, and free exhibitions.
M: You trust government committees more than I do.
W: I trust public access more than exclusive membership.
M: At least we agree that children should experience art early. My school had no music room, and I assumed creativity was something other families did.
W: The same happened to me. Early exposure gives children permission to experiment before embarrassment and exam pressure take over.
M: Do you think access will actually improve?
W: Yes. In the future, art will become more accessible through community studios, digital collections, and tools that let people create without expensive equipment.
M: Technology can distribute images, but it does not guarantee understanding.
W: No, which is why investment in teachers and local spaces still matters.
M: Then perhaps the problem is not whether art is naturally exclusive, but who pays to open the door.
W: Exactly, and society can choose to open it wider. **Explanation summary** *User-provided Part 3 topic version for The Arts ( version 2 ). Check the statement-level explanations for the final speaker mapping.*

<!-- word_comment_id: 494; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: Topic: The Arts (Version 2) (2026) -->

#### Topic: The Arts (Version 1) (2026)

<!-- group_key: listening-p3-set035 -->
<!-- audio_source_key: listening-p3-set035-audio -->
<!-- transcript_status: source_word_comment -->
<!-- word_comment_ids: 495 -->
<details class="audio-transcript">

W: The community theatre says everyone is welcome, but tickets, transport, and childcare make that promise unrealistic. Art is still mainly suitable for privileged people with spare money and time.
M: I would not call art mainly a privilege; people create songs, stories, and images without entering a theatre. Still, public policy should remove practical barriers. The government should invest more in the arts, especially outside major cities.
W: I would rather rely on local groups than government programmes. Funding buildings is not enough if people feel the building is not for them.
M: That is why children should be exposed to art early. A child who performs, paints, or visits a museum at school is less likely to see culture as somebody else's world.
W: I agree. My daughter was nervous before her first workshop, and now she writes scenes for her friends.
M: Those early experiences can last for life.
W: But what about adults who missed them?
M: Technology will make art more accessible in the future. Local performances can be streamed, archives can be opened, and inexpensive tools can support new creators.
W: That helps, although live participation still needs affordable spaces.
M: Of course. Digital access should expand the audience, not justify closing theatres.
W: Then schools, local groups, and technology all have roles.
M: Different doors into the same house. **Explanation summary** *User-provided Part 3 topic version for The Arts ( version 1 ). Check the statement-level explanations for the final speaker mapping.*

#### Topic: Music And Singer (Version 3) (2026)

<!-- group_key: listening-p3-set036 -->
<!-- audio_source_key: listening-p3-set036-audio -->
<!-- transcript_status: source_word_comment -->
<!-- word_comment_ids: 496 -->
<details class="audio-transcript">
<summary>Audio transcript</summary>

W: The documentary follows a singer who returned to her old school and paid for instruments. Watching the students, I can understand why singers can be good role models for young people.
M: That was generous, but one carefully filmed act does not make singers reliable role models; documentaries select the most flattering parts of a life.
W: True, but responsible artists can still demonstrate discipline and generosity.
M: Our editor hates her music.
W: Taste in music is highly personal. I love her voice, you prefer jazz, and the editor listens to electronic noise.
M: Exactly. Musical taste is highly personal because a song carries memories that another listener does not share.
W: Does that weaken the idea that music crosses cultures?
M: Not for me. Music is a universal language because rhythm, tension, and release can communicate before anyone translates the words.
W: I think cultural knowledge still matters, so I would not go that far.
M: Fair enough. But our film proves that music can manipulate feelings. The same classroom scene feels hopeful with piano and uncomfortable with low electronic sounds.
W: Absolutely. We tested both versions, and viewers described completely different emotions.
M: That power is why filmmakers choose soundtracks so carefully.
W: And why audiences should notice when music is guiding their judgement. **Explanation summary** *User-provided Part 3 topic version for Music And Singer ( version 3 ). Check the statement-level explanations for the final speaker mapping.*

<!-- word_comment_id: 496; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: Topic: Music And Singer (Version 3) (2026) -->

</details>

|---|---|
<!-- source_key: listening-p3-set036-q001 -->

<!-- source_key: listening-p3-set036-q002 -->
| 2. Taste in music is a highly personal thing. | B |
<!-- source_key: listening-p3-set036-q003 -->
| 3. Music is a universal language. | M |
<!-- source_key: listening-p3-set036-q004 -->

#### Topic: Music And Singer (Version 2) (2026)

<!-- group_key: listening-p3-set037 -->
<!-- audio_source_key: listening-p3-set037-audio -->
<!-- transcript_status: source_word_comment -->
<!-- word_comment_ids: 497 -->
<details class="audio-transcript">
<summary>Audio transcript</summary>

W: You have changed the radio station six times since we left the city.
M: Because musical taste is highly personal. Your cheerful pop songs make me tired, while this old blues recording keeps me awake.
W: I was about to say the opposite, but I do not think taste is purely personal; families, age groups, and cultures shape what we learn to enjoy. Does that mean music cannot connect people?
M: No. I still think music is a universal language. At the wedding in Morocco, I understood none of the lyrics, yet the rhythm told everyone when to clap and dance.
W: Maybe, but shared movement does not mean shared meaning.
M: Meaning can differ while emotion still travels.
W: How do you feel about singers as role models? My niece copied a singer's charity challenge and raised money for an animal shelter.
M: That is a good outcome, but I would not automatically treat celebrities as examples for children.
W: Neither would I, though some use their influence responsibly.
M: The music makes an ordinary car sound like an adventure.
W: Yes, and the sad song in that radio appeal makes listeners feel guilty. Music can manipulate emotions very effectively.
M: We definitely agree there.
W: Fine. Keep the blues, but only until the next service station. **Explanation summary** *User-provided Part 3 topic version for Music And Singer ( version 2 ). Check the statement-level explanations for the final speaker mapping.*

#### Topic: Music And Singer (Version 1) (2026)

<!-- group_key: listening-p3-set038 -->
<!-- audio_source_key: listening-p3-set038-audio -->
<!-- transcript_status: source_word_comment -->
<!-- word_comment_ids: 498 -->
<details class="audio-transcript">

M: The charity concert organiser wants a famous singer to visit the children's ward. I think singers can be excellent role models when they show young fans that success can include generosity.
W: Possibly, although the children may admire the fame more than the generosity.
M: That depends on how the visit is presented.
W: My concern is the playlist. You chose rock, but musical taste is highly personal. Some children will want dance music, others traditional songs, and some may prefer silence.
M: We can invite requests.
W: Good. Music can still connect them even when they do not share a language. I see it as a universal language because melody communicates comfort without explanation.
M: I am not sure it is completely universal; cultural associations can change the message.
W: They can, but the basic human response remains.
M: The hospital psychologist also warned us not to use overly sad songs.
W: That makes sense. Music can manipulate people's feelings, especially when they are already vulnerable.
M: Yes. An uplifting song can create courage, while the wrong one can deepen fear.
W: So we should use that power carefully rather than pretending the concert is emotionally neutral. **Explanation summary** *User-provided Part 3 topic version for Music And Singer ( version 1 ). Check the statement-level explanations for the final speaker mapping.*

#### Topic: The Local Central ( version 1 )

<!-- group_key: listening-p3-set039 -->
<!-- audio_source_key: listening-p3-set039-audio -->
<!-- transcript_status: source_word_comment -->
<!-- word_comment_ids: 499 -->
<details class="audio-transcript">
<summary>Audio transcript</summary>

M: The local centre has only one exhibition room, so repeating the usual farming tools and old photographs would waste the opportunity. Exhibitions should be different and diverse-oral histories one month, contemporary craft the next, then work from recent migrants.
W: Variety is useful, but I am more worried about what those exhibitions would reveal. Traditional customs are gradually losing their significance. My nieces know the festival costumes but not why they are worn.
M: Some customs change rather than disappear.
W: The harvest festival may disappear in the near future. Attendance has fallen for five years, and the youngest volunteers are already in their forties.
M: That sounds serious, but a centre could help renew interest.
W: Only if young people are involved before adulthood.
M: Schools are important in shaping future generations. Teachers can connect local history with music, food, design, and family stories.
W: I completely agree. A single school project can send children home inviting grandparents to share stories that nobody has heard for years.
M: Then the centre should partner with schools rather than wait for students to visit independently.
W: And give them space to reinterpret tradition, not just memorise it. **Explanation summary** *User-provided Part 3 topic version for The Local Central ( version 1 ). Check the statement-level explanations for the final speaker mapping.*

<!-- word_comment_id: 499; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: Topic: The Local Central ( version 1 ) -->

<!-- source_key: listening-p3-set039-q003 -->
| 3. Local festivals will disappear in the near future. | W |

#### Topic: The Local Central ( version 2 ) ( 2026 )

<!-- group_key: listening-p3-set040 -->
<!-- audio_source_key: listening-p3-set040-audio -->
<!-- transcript_status: source_word_comment -->
<!-- word_comment_ids: 500 -->
<details class="audio-transcript">
<summary>Audio transcript</summary>

W: The heritage fair committee has collected dozens of objects, but right now every table looks the same.
M: Then the exhibitions need to be different and diverse. One area could use sound recordings, another could recreate a shop, and another could let visitors handle replicas instead of reading labels.
W: I am less concerned about variety than meaning. Traditional customs are gradually losing significance because people encounter them as static objects rather than living practices.
M: I agree. Even I recognised the wedding drum but could not explain the ceremony it belonged to.
W: The winter lantern festival worries me most. With fewer organisers and rising costs, I think it will disappear in the near future.
M: I hope not. A smaller festival might survive in a new form.
W: Perhaps, but waiting for volunteers will not be enough. Schools are important in shaping future generations, and they should teach children how these events connect to local identity.
M: I do not think schools should carry that role. Families and community groups, rather than classrooms, should shape children's connection to tradition.
W: Certainly. I am saying schools should take the lead because they can reach every child.
M: Then the centre should invite young visitors directly instead of relying on classrooms to do the work.

#### Topic: Local Cultural Differences - Version 2 (2026)

<!-- group_key: listening-p3-set041 -->
<!-- audio_source_key: listening-p3-set041-audio -->
<!-- transcript_status: source_word_comment -->
<!-- word_comment_ids: 501 -->
<details class="audio-transcript">
<summary>Audio transcript</summary>

M: Have you ever noticed how different local cultures can be, and how easily people end up misunderstanding local customs and traditions?
W: Yes, absolutely. People misinterpret regional traditions all the time, which causes a lot of confusion.
M: True. But don't you think the media sometimes exaggerate the extent of cultural differences? Most places share similar core values anyway.
W: I completely agree with you on that. They always blow things out of proportion for drama.
M: And it's not just a social issue. Look at the corporate world - international businesses frequently struggle with cultural differences during global expansion.
M: Anyway, with all these changes happening, do you think local heritage will completely disappear?
W: Not really. I think traditional customs and local practices are more likely to adapt and survive through modern technology.

<!-- word_comment_id: 501; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: Topic: Local Cultural Differences - Version 2 (2026) -->
