#### Topic: Homeschooling (2026)

<!-- group_key: listening-p3-set042 -->
<!-- audio_source_key: listening-p3-set042-audio -->
<!-- transcript_status: source_word_comment -->
<!-- word_comment_ids: 502 -->
<details class="audio-transcript">
<summary>Audio transcript</summary>

W: I understand why home education appeals to some parents. In a large class, even a good teacher has to move at a pace that suits most pupils. At home, a child can spend longer on difficult subjects and move faster through material they already understand.
M: That may improve academic progress, but I worry about what happens beyond lessons. School teaches children to cooperate, manage disagreements, and build relationships with people from different backgrounds. A weekly sports club may help, but it is not the same as spending every day with classmates.
W: Perhaps, although homeschooled children are not automatically isolated. They can join music groups, community projects, and online classes. A flexible timetable can also support children with health conditions, families who travel, or young people training seriously in sport.
M: Flexibility is useful, but parents need time, patience, and enough subject knowledge. Good intentions do not always produce effective teaching.
W: I agree. I would not present homeschooling as a solution for every household.
M: Nor would I reject it completely. Its success depends on the child's needs, the support available, and the of the education.

<!-- word_comment_id: 502; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: Topic: Homeschooling (2026) -->

</details>

| Item | Answer / matching detail |
|---|---|
<!-- source_key: listening-p3-set042-q001 -->
| 1. Adjusting the pace of lessons may help children learn more efficiently. | W |
<!-- source_key: listening-p3-set042-q002 -->
| 2. Occasional group activities may not completely replace regular contact with classmates. | M |
<!-- source_key: listening-p3-set042-q003 -->
| 3. Home education can benefit children whose circumstances make a fixed school timetable difficult. | W |
<!-- source_key: listening-p3-set042-q004 -->
| 4. The success of homeschooling depends on whether a family can provide suitable teaching and support. | B |

#### Topic: Work Business / Business and Cultural

<!-- group_key: listening-p3-set043 -->
<!-- audio_source_key: listening-p3-set043-audio -->
<!-- transcript_status: source_word_comment -->
<!-- word_comment_ids: 503 -->
<details class="audio-transcript">
<summary>Audio transcript</summary>

W: Many people assume that a new business needs a large amount of money to succeed. However, a friend of mine started a small design company in her spare room. She used borrowed computers and had very little start-up capital. By concentrating on one service and listening carefully to customers, she managed to open two branches within two years.
M: That is an impressive achievement, but I would be cautious about expanding so Without enough money in reserve, a delayed payment or an unexpected bill could prevent a company from paying its employees. In my view, a business should establish some financial security before opening another branch.
W: That is a reasonable point. Companies also need to consider cultural differences when entering a foreign market. Communication styles vary widely. A direct comment may appear honest and efficient in one country but rude in another.
M: I completely agree. Understanding local customs helps businesses gain trust and avoid unnecessary misunderstandings. Companies should listen to local employees rather than depending on broad stereotypes.
W: Workplace culture is important inside the company too. A less formal atmosphere can make junior employees feel comfortable enough to raise concerns before minor issues develop into serious problems.
M: Possibly, although employees still need clear responsibilities as the company grows.
W: Certainly. Open communication and a clear structure can exist together.

<!-- word_comment_id: 503; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: Topic: Work Business / Business and Cultural -->

</details>

| Item | Answer / matching detail |
|---|---|
<!-- source_key: listening-p3-set043-q001 -->
| 1. A business should build up financial reserves before expanding. | M |
<!-- source_key: listening-p3-set043-q002 -->
| 2. Companies working in another country need to understand local customs and communication styles. | B |
<!-- source_key: listening-p3-set043-q003 -->
| 3. Having very little starting capital does not necessarily prevent a small company from growing. | W |
<!-- source_key: listening-p3-set043-q004 -->
| 4. A less formal workplace may encourage employees to mention problems earlier. | W |

#### Topic: Homeschooling – Version 2 (2026)

<!-- group_key: listening-p3-set044 -->
<!-- audio_source_key: listening-p3-set044-audio -->
<!-- transcript_status: source_word_comment -->
<!-- word_comment_ids: 504 -->
<details class="audio-transcript">
<summary>Audio transcript</summary>

W: One advantage of homeschooling is that learning does not have to be limited to a classroom. Parents can use museums, local events, or practical activities as part of a child's education. For some children, connecting lessons with real situations can make difficult ideas easier to understand.
M: That can be valuable, although I think parents sometimes underestimate how demanding home education can become. Planning lessons is only one part of it. They also have to check progress, find appropriate materials, and recognise when a child needs help that they themselves cannot provide.
W: That's true. On the other hand, studying at home can be particularly helpful for children who find the normal school environment difficult. A setting and fewer interruptions may allow them to work with greater confidence, especially if they need more time to process information.
M: My other concern is that children need experience dealing with people outside their immediate family. They will eventually have to work with others, accept different opinions, and cope with situations they cannot completely control.
W: Certainly, although school is not the only place where those skills can develop. Homeschooling families can arrange regular activities with other children, but parents do need to make a deliberate effort to create those opportunities.
M: Exactly. So I think the important is not simply whether homeschooling is good or bad. Families have to consider what the individual child requires and whether they have the resources to meet those needs.
W: Yes. What works extremely well for one child could be completely unsuitable for another.

<!-- word_comment_id: 504; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: Topic: Homeschooling – Version 2 (2026) -->

</details>

| Item | Answer / matching detail |
|---|---|
<!-- source_key: listening-p3-set044-q001 -->
| 1. Providing home education may involve more responsibilities than some parents initially expect. | M |
<!-- source_key: listening-p3-set044-q002 -->
| 2. Some children may learn more effectively when they are removed from the distractions of a conventional classroom. | W |
<!-- source_key: listening-p3-set044-q003 -->
| 3. Experiences outside formal lessons can help children understand what they are studying. | W |
<!-- source_key: listening-p3-set044-q004 -->
| 4. Whether homeschooling is appropriate depends partly on the circumstances and requirements of each child. | B |

### **Part 4: Longer Monologues - Questions 16–17**

> **Topic: A newly released novel (2026)**
>
> <!-- audio_source_key: listening-p4-set001-audio -->
> <!-- transcript_status: source_word_comment -->
> <!-- word_comment_ids: 505 -->
> <details class="audio-transcript">
> <summary>Audio transcript</summary>
>
> Good evening. Tonight I'm reviewing James Parker's latest novel, Shadow in the Night. Readers who know Parker from gentle romances such as Whispers of the Heart may be surprised. The new book leaves those love stories behind and moves into suspense, secrets, and psychological tension. In other words, it represents a clear departure from his earlier work rather than simply being another soft romantic novel. The change is ambitious, and several scenes are genuinely gripping, although not every critic believes the mystery is handled equally well. Some fans want Parker to continue writing thrillers, while others would prefer him to return immediately to romance. I would not recommend either extreme. Before beginning his next book, he should study the reviewers' comments carefully and decide which elements worked and which did not. Their advice could help him combine emotional depth with a stronger, more controlled plot. Whatever direction he chooses, this novel proves that he is willing to experiment rather than repeat a familiar formula.
>
> <!-- word_comment_id: 505; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: Topic: A newly released novel (2026) -->
>
> </details>

>
<!-- source_key: listening-p4-set001-q001 -->
> **16.1. What does the announcer say about the new novel?**
>
> <u>A. It is different from his earlier works</u>
>
> B. It is romantic and soft
>
> C. It is less famous than his earlier works
>
<!-- source_key: listening-p4-set001-q002 -->
> **16.2. What does the announcer say the writer should do in the future?**
>

> **Topic: Professionalism at work (2026)**
>
> <!-- audio_source_key: listening-p4-set002-audio -->
> <!-- transcript_status: source_word_comment -->
> <!-- word_comment_ids: 506 -->
> <details class="audio-transcript">
> <summary>Audio transcript</summary>
>
> Good morning. When people hear the word professionalism, they often think first about formal clothes, impressive language, or creating a polished image. Those things may influence how others see us, but they are not the centre of professional behaviour. In my view, the foundation is maintaining a positive attitude: being approachable, open-minded, dependable, and willing to help when colleagues face difficulties. Such an attitude may create a better working environment and make a good impression, but those are results rather than the main principle itself. We should also remember that professionalism is not a fixed concept. Forty years ago, it was commonly associated with strict rules, hierarchy, and appearance. Today, workplaces expect adaptability, cultural awareness, digital confidence, and the ability to cooperate with people from different backgrounds. As technology and working patterns continue to develop, our understanding of professionalism is changing too. It would therefore be a mistake to assume that the definition is exactly the same as it was in the past or that it will never change again.
>
> <!-- word_comment_id: 506; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: Topic: Professionalism at work (2026) -->
>
> </details>

> **Topic: Writer's block (2026)**
>
> <!-- audio_source_key: listening-p4-set003-audio -->
> <!-- transcript_status: source_word_comment -->
> <!-- word_comment_ids: 507 -->
> <details class="audio-transcript">
> <summary>Audio transcript</summary>
>
> When I was writing my latest novel, I tried almost every piece of advice people give to writers. Some friends suggested writing for fifteen or twenty minutes every single day, while others said I simply needed a perfectly room. Both ideas can help, but neither suited me consistently. What made the biggest difference was creating dedicated periods for writing and protecting those periods from meetings, messages, and household tasks. I did not write at exactly the same hour every day; instead, I chose longer blocks that matched my energy and working style. The most difficult stage came when I experienced serious writer's block. I kept telling myself that a professional writer should solve the problem alone, so I refused to seek advice from others. Looking back, that is what I regret most. I did not ignore an editor's feedback, and the problem was not that I lacked a plan. I simply waited too long before speaking to fellow writers and mentors, whose outside perspective might have helped me move forward much sooner.
>
> <!-- word_comment_id: 507; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: Topic: Writer's block (2026) -->
>
> </details>

> **Topic: Work from home (2026)**
>
> <!-- audio_source_key: listening-p4-set004-audio -->
> <!-- transcript_status: source_word_comment -->
> <!-- word_comment_ids: 508 -->
> <details class="audio-transcript">
> <summary>Audio transcript</summary>
>
> Before I began working from home, I imagined that it would be completely peaceful. I thought the absence of colleagues chatting nearby would mean there were no distractions at all. In reality, the interruptions simply changed: laundry, deliveries, dishes, and family members can be just as distracting as an office conversation. I still enjoy controlling my schedule and avoiding the daily commute, but overall the experience has not been as good as I expected. The loneliness is more noticeable than I anticipated, and although video calls are useful, I would never say they are better than face-to-face discussion. People often ask whether they need a large, expensive home office. They do not; a well-organised corner can be enough. What matters more is self-motivation, clear boundaries, and the type of person you are. Some workers become highly productive at home, while others struggle without routine and social contact. So the success of remote work depends largely on an individual's situation and personality, not on one universal rule.
>
> <!-- word_comment_id: 508; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: Topic: Work from home (2026) -->
>
> </details>

> **Topic: The importance of sleep (2026)**

> Sleep advice appears everywhere now, from television programmes to social media posts. Some reports suggest that everyone needs exactly the same number of hours, while others warn that modern sleep has collapsed completely. In my opinion, the media often overemphasise the subject, turning small research findings into alarming headlines. That does not mean sleep is unimportant, but the public conversation can create unnecessary anxiety. For practical improvement, experts repeatedly return to one basic point: blocking out noise and light is the key to sleeping well. A dark, room helps the brain reduce stimulation and makes it easier to enter deeper stages of sleep. A comfortable bed may help, and getting enough rest obviously matters, but neither statement is as specific as the environmental advice. Young people are sometimes criticised for using phones late at night, yet that is not the main public attitude I am discussing. The larger problem is that sleep has become so heavily discussed that people may start worrying about every imperfect night instead of developing calm, consistent habits.
>

> **Topic: Advertising and sponsoring (2026)**
>
> <!-- audio_source_key: listening-p4-set007-audio -->
> <!-- transcript_status: source_word_comment -->
> <!-- word_comment_ids: 511 -->
> <details class="audio-transcript">
> <summary>Audio transcript</summary>
>
> Advertising can introduce a product or programme to people who might otherwise never discover it, but constant promotion also has a negative side. Television networks sometimes repeat trailers so frequently that viewers become tired of a series before they have even watched it. In that sense, series can be damaged by overexposure: excessive publicity replaces curiosity with irritation. Repetition can certainly be annoying, but the broader issue is the harm done to the programme's image, not the claim that advertising costs as much as producing a film. Sponsorship in sport creates a different risk. A company may gain visibility by attaching its name to a tournament, yet it also becomes associated with whatever happens there. If athletes cheat, organisers behave badly, or a scandal dominates the event, the sponsor's campaign can generate negative publicity for the sport itself. Advertising may sometimes attract fans or support ticket and merchandise sales, but those positive possibilities do not remove the reputational danger. Companies should therefore consider not only how many people will see a campaign, but also what kind of association the public may remember.
>
> <!-- word_comment_id: 511; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: Topic: Advertising and sponsoring (2026) -->
>
> </details>

> The Silver Horizon is visually impressive, and its locations are carefully chosen, so the setting is not the problem. The original cast also remained in place throughout production. What disappointed me was the dialogue, which often sounds unrealistic. Characters who are supposed to be close friends speak in stiff, formal sentences, and an emotional exchange between the hero and his mentor feels more like a rehearsed stage scene than a natural conversation. This weakness repeatedly pulls the audience out of the story. I believe the problem reflects a wider change in the film industry. Studios increasingly demand scripts that can be completed , adjusted to current trends, and approved by several marketing departments. Those modern industry pressures are negatively influencing script production because writers have less time to develop believable voices and layered relationships. I am not predicting that technology will replace actors, and simply inventing more plot ideas would not solve the underlying issue. The industry needs to give writers sufficient time and creative space; otherwise, even attractive films will continue to feel rushed and emotionally unconvincing.

> I recently visited La Bella Bistro, a restaurant with an attractive location and a warm-looking dining room. I waited a long time before anyone took my order, water arrived only after I asked twice, and the staff appeared too busy to explain the menu. When they finally spoke to us, the interaction felt rushed and impersonal. This matters more than ever because customers can now order good food at home with very little effort. A restaurant cannot compete with online delivery merely by offering organic ingredients or handing people a ready-made package. Its real advantage is the human experience. Guests should feel valued and welcome from the moment they enter: a friendly greeting, attentive service, and genuine interest can turn an ordinary meal into an occasion. La Bella Bistro already has a pleasant setting and acceptable food, but unless it improves the way customers are treated, people may decide that staying home is easier and more enjoyable.

> **Topic: Famous writers (2026)**
>
> <!-- audio_source_key: listening-p4-set010-audio -->
> <!-- transcript_status: source_word_comment -->
> <!-- word_comment_ids: 514 -->
> <details class="audio-transcript">
> <summary>Audio transcript</summary>
>
> Today I want to compare two influential writers who came from different countries and did not work in exactly the same genre. Academics have certainly not overlooked them; both have been studied for decades, and there is no useful basis for claiming that one was simply less successful than the other. What is particularly interesting is their literary relationship. They make references to each other's work, sometimes borrowing an image, responding to an idea, or mentioning a character that readers recognise from the other author. These connections show mutual respect and create an additional layer for informed readers. Their writing, however, is rarely straightforward. Symbols, unreliable narrators, and indirect arguments mean that the meanings are not always easily identified. A reader may understand the basic story on the first reading but miss the social or philosophical message underneath it. Their reputation may indeed extend beyond the original audience, but that is not the feature being emphasised here. The central point is that interpretation requires patience, comparison, and sometimes more than one reading.
>
> <!-- word_comment_id: 514; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: Topic: Famous writers (2026) -->
>
> </details>

> **Topic: Sports in education**
>
> <!-- audio_source_key: listening-p4-set011-audio -->
> <!-- transcript_status: source_word_comment -->
> <!-- word_comment_ids: 515 -->
> <details class="audio-transcript">
> <summary>Audio transcript</summary>
>
> Schools often discuss sport as though competition were automatically beneficial. Supporters say tournaments can attract investment or help identify future professional athletes, but those are not my main concerns. Constantly ranking children and comparing one student with another can cause harmful effects. A child who is repeatedly treated as a loser may avoid physical activity altogether, while even successful students can become anxious about performance. This does not mean schools should remove sport. On the contrary, physical activity teaches cooperation, communication, resilience, and healthy habits that ordinary classroom lessons cannot provide. My advice is to use sport to provide students with balance in their lives. Academic work demands long periods of concentration and sitting, so movement and social activity offer an essential counterweight. I am not arguing that sport must become a rigid mandatory subject in every circumstance, nor should schools keep students focused only on academic subjects. The better approach is to reduce unnecessary comparison, create inclusive activities, and allow sport to support physical, social, and emotional development.
>
> <!-- word_comment_id: 515; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: Topic: Sports in education -->
>
> </details>

> Good afternoon. People often try to save by waiting until the end of the month and putting aside whatever remains. The problem is that unexpected expenses usually consume that money first. A more reliable method is to save a fixed amount regularly, for example by arranging an automatic monthly transfer as soon as income arrives. It does not need to be a huge daily payment, and using credit cards is not a saving strategy; credit can easily hide how much has actually been spent. Consistency matters more than one dramatic sacrifice. Managing money also becomes easier when people seek guidance. Someone with experience, such as a financial adviser, a knowledgeable family member, or a trusted mentor, can identify mistakes that a beginner may not notice. Keeping every saving in a single account is not always suitable, and avoiding long-term planning would make future goals harder to achieve. People can manage money more successfully when they get advice from people who have experience, review their decisions, and adjust their habits. Saving successfully is therefore a combination of regular action and informed advice.
>

> **Topic: Security cameras (2026)**
>
> <!-- audio_source_key: listening-p4-set016-audio -->
> <!-- transcript_status: source_word_comment -->

> <details class="audio-transcript">

> Several colleagues became nervous when security cameras were installed in our office. They felt constantly watched and assumed management no longer trusted them. I understand the reaction, but I think people are unnecessarily worried about the cameras. They are placed in shared areas such as entrances, corridors, and storage rooms, not in private spaces. Their purpose is to discourage theft, clarify what happened during an accident, and protect both employees and visitors. It is not true that most people are unaware of the cameras; everyone knows they are there. Nor am I arguing for complete coverage of every corner. Appropriate limits and clear policies are important. Once staff understand the purpose, they should feel reassured about the cameras' presence rather than intimidated by it. A recording helped us recover a missing bag last year, and it also showed that an employee had not caused an incident for which he was initially blamed. Cameras are not useful only after something goes wrong; their visibility can prevent problems as well. Used responsibly, they support safety without becoming unnecessary surveillance.
>
> <!-- word_comment_id: 520; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: Topic: Security cameras (2026) -->

> I have just finished a biography of the physicist Elena Maric, and the writing makes it genuinely exciting to read. The author does not turn the book into a technical textbook, even when discussing difficult theories. Instead, scientific discoveries are connected to personal conflicts, travel, and moments of doubt, so the reader understands why each breakthrough mattered. Complex ideas are explained in simple language, which means the overall content has been written for a general audience. Experts may still appreciate the historical detail, but specialist knowledge is not required. The book is therefore very different from a work focused only on equations or laboratory procedures. Some biographies become dry because they list dates without creating a story; this one avoids that problem by combining clear explanation with strong narrative movement. It has already attracted readers who would not normally choose a science book. Its success shows that accuracy and accessibility do not have to be opposites: a writer can respect the subject while still making the life and ideas of a scientist understandable and engaging.

> **Topic: A research into happiness (2026)**

> <!-- audio_source_key: listening-p4-set018-audio -->

> <details class="audio-transcript">
> <summary>Audio transcript</summary>
>
> Good morning, listeners. A recent study on happiness has received enormous media attention, but much of the coverage has not been accurate. Several headlines reduced a complicated set of findings to claims such as "one habit guarantees happiness," even though the researchers made no such promise. The study has certainly not been ignored, and it would be wrong to say that reporting has been consistently reliable. Sensational summaries have left many readers with a distorted impression of what the evidence actually shows. There is a second limitation to remember. Researchers can identify patterns involving relationships, health, work, and security, but it is unlikely that they will find one conclusive answer that applies to every person. This does not mean individuals cannot find personal happiness, nor does it prevent the subject from being discussed in the media. It simply reflects the fact that happiness is influenced by values, culture, circumstances, and personality. Instead of searching for a universal formula, people should treat research as guidance and consider which findings are relevant to their own lives.
>
> <!-- word_comment_id: 522; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: Topic: A research into happiness (2026) -->
>
> </details>

> **Topic: Setting goals (2026)**
>
> <!-- audio_source_key: listening-p4-set019-audio -->
> <!-- transcript_status: source_word_comment -->
> <!-- word_comment_ids: 523 -->
> <details class="audio-transcript">
> <summary>Audio transcript</summary>
>
> Short-term goals are sometimes criticised for being rigid. People imagine that once a target is written down, every other opportunity must be ignored. In practice, a short-term goal can help people adjust their priorities when circumstances change. If work suddenly becomes busy, for example, a plan to exercise five times a week can be revised to three realistic sessions rather than abandoned completely. That flexibility is different from having no direction at all. Goal setting also helps prevent mistakes. Clear objectives encourage people to think ahead, notice conflicts, and choose actions that support the result they actually want. Too many poorly organised goals may cause confusion, but that is a problem of selection, not proof that planning itself is harmful. Goals can create opportunities, yet the point I am stressing is their role in reducing careless decisions. A useful system combines direction with regular review: decide what matters, break it into manageable steps, and change those steps when new information appears. The goal guides decisions without becoming a prison.
>
> <!-- word_comment_id: 523; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: Topic: Setting goals (2026) -->
>
> </details>

> **Topic: A break from studying (2026)**

> <details class="audio-transcript">

>
> After finishing school, I did not go directly to college because I was not ready to start higher education. My grades were sufficient, and although tuition was a concern, money was not the main reason for delaying. I felt exhausted by years of examinations and wanted time to decide what I actually hoped to study. I chose to travel for two years, but the purpose was not simply to avoid education or to travel without direction. I wanted to become more independent. During the trip, I worked temporary jobs, arranged accommodation, managed a limited budget, and solved problems without relying on my family. Those experiences made me more confident and also clarified the subjects that interested me. By the time I returned, university felt like a decision I had made for myself rather than the automatic next step expected by other people. A break from study can become wasted time if it has no purpose, but in my case travelling developed responsibility and prepared me to approach higher education with greater maturity.
>

> **Topic: Learning and development**
>
> <!-- audio_source_key: listening-p4-set022-audio -->

> <details class="audio-transcript">
> <summary>Audio transcript</summary>
>
> In fast-moving industries, professional development cannot be reduced to memorising information or following instructions that never change. The real aim is to learn how to work effectively. That means identifying high-impact tasks, organising processes clearly, and judging whether an activity produces a useful result rather than simply keeping us busy. Working independently can sometimes be valuable, but refusing all guidance is inefficient, and strict fixed instructions may become outdated. Effectiveness provides a foundation, yet it is not enough for long-term progress. People also need to be willing to experiment. Trying a new workflow, testing an unfamiliar tool, or approaching a problem from a different angle may lead to failure at first, but those attempts create learning that routine cannot provide. Maintaining a predictable daily pattern feels safe, and avoiding risk may prevent small mistakes, but both can limit innovation. Professionals who remain relevant are usually those who combine disciplined work with curiosity: they evaluate results, learn from unsuccessful trials, and keep adjusting their methods as the industry develops.
>
> <!-- word_comment_id: 526; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: Topic: Learning and development -->
>

> **Topic: The history of railways**
>
> <!-- audio_source_key: listening-p4-set023-audio -->
> <!-- transcript_status: source_word_comment -->
> <!-- word_comment_ids: 527 -->
> <details class="audio-transcript">
> <summary>Audio transcript</summary>
>
> The railway network entered a serious crisis during the middle of the twentieth century. Passenger numbers fell as private cars became more common, while freight companies increasingly chose trucks. The major problem was financial: the railways were not making enough money to cover wages, maintenance, fuel, and the replacement of ageing equipment. A shortage of engineers created difficulties in some regions, and bad weather occasionally damaged tracks, but neither was the central threat to the whole system. Faced with falling revenue, the leader in charge could have suspended services or sold major assets. Instead, he decided to continue policies that had already been implemented. Management maintained existing routes, pricing, and investment priorities in the hope that economic conditions would improve. This cautious decision avoided immediate disruption, but it also delayed modernisation. Critics later argued that preserving the status allowed competitors to move further ahead. The episode illustrates how an organisation can recognise a financial problem yet still respond by repeating familiar policies rather than making the structural changes that the situation may require.
>
> <!-- word_comment_id: 527; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: Topic: The history of railways -->
>
> </details>

>

> **Topic: The environment**
>
> <!-- audio_source_key: listening-p4-set024-audio -->
> <!-- transcript_status: source_word_comment -->
> <!-- word_comment_ids: 528 -->
> <details class="audio-transcript">
> <summary>Audio transcript</summary>
>
> Protecting the environment can sound like a task only governments and large industries can influence, but everyday behaviour matters as well. People should make a daily effort to recycle, reduce unnecessary waste, and use energy carefully. These actions are more realistic than demanding that all factories move to rural areas or that every private vehicle be replaced immediately by an electric bicycle. One household's contribution may appear small, but millions of repeated choices can reduce pressure on natural resources. At the same time, individual habits cannot excuse destructive activity on a larger scale. Deforestation removes habitats and weakens forests that absorb carbon and regulate water. Human activities like this harm nature and contribute to climate change and health problems, especially when smoke, flooding, or poor water affects nearby communities. They do not create stronger ecosystems, and any short-term economic benefit must be weighed against long-term damage. Environmental protection therefore requires both personal consistency and serious action against practices that destroy the systems on which communities depend.
>
> <!-- word_comment_id: 528; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: Topic: The environment -->
>
> </details>

>

> **Topic: Building design**
>
> <!-- audio_source_key: listening-p4-set025-audio -->

> <details class="audio-transcript">
> <summary>Audio transcript</summary>
>
> Modern buildings are often immediately recognisable because they are closely associated with contemporary cityscapes. Glass walls, straight lines, steel frames, and limited decoration connect them visually with business districts and rapidly developing urban centres. They are not notable for prioritising traditional materials, and they are certainly not mainly constructed in rural environments. Their appearance reflects the wider identity of the modern city. Yet this consistency creates a problem. Many new buildings display a lack of ambition. Developers frequently choose designs that are cheap, to approve, and easy to reproduce, rather than exploring bold forms or imaginative public spaces. As a result, one office block may look almost identical to another in a different country. This does not represent the peak of architectural innovation, nor does it show a strong commitment to maximising green areas. Practical requirements are unavoidable, but efficiency should not become an excuse for visual sameness. Cities need buildings that function well while also responding creatively to local history, climate, and community life.
>
> <!-- word_comment_id: 529; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: Topic: Building design -->
>
> </details>

> **Topic: Concentration**
>
> <!-- audio_source_key: listening-p4-set026-audio -->
> <!-- transcript_status: source_word_comment -->
> <!-- word_comment_ids: 530 -->
> <details class="audio-transcript">
> <summary>Audio transcript</summary>
>
> People often treat concentration as a test of personal discipline. When attention disappears, they blame themselves and assume that trying harder will solve the problem. Individual effort does matter, but research suggests that concentration depends mostly on the environment. Lighting, background noise, temperature, phone notifications, and even the arrangement of a desk can either support or interrupt sustained attention. External distractions are part of that environment, but the issue is broader than a single noise or interruption. Another fact is commonly missed: concentration has limits. The brain cannot maintain the same of focus for endless hours, yet many people work without breaks because they believe stopping shows weakness. In reality, people often fail to consider the limits of concentration. They do not consistently recognise those limits, and most have not mastered the skill of managing them. After a certain point, errors increase and productivity falls. A sensible approach is to improve the physical setting, protect periods of focused work, and take deliberate breaks before mental energy is completely exhausted.
>
> <!-- word_comment_id: 530; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: Topic: Concentration -->
>
> </details>

>

> **Topic: Customers in business**
>
> <!-- audio_source_key: listening-p4-set027-audio -->
> <!-- transcript_status: source_word_comment -->
> <!-- word_comment_ids: 531 -->
> <details class="audio-transcript">
> <summary>Audio transcript</summary>
>
> Companies often identify a small group of important customers, sometimes called key accounts. Their value goes far beyond making one large purchase. They provide steady revenue, influence other buyers through recommendations, and offer feedback that helps a business improve its services. They do not normally hire or train the company's employees, and even the strongest customer cannot guarantee that stock prices will never fall. Their importance comes from a combination of income, advocacy, and useful information. Businesses must also accept that customer behaviour is unpredictable. Preferences can change because of technology, economic pressure, or a new competitor. The correct response is not to reduce marketing automatically or standardise every product while ignoring individual needs. Companies should monitor customer habits and update their services when necessary. This may involve adjusting communication, redesigning a feature, or introducing a more flexible delivery option. A firm that listens and adapts can preserve trust, whereas a rigid company may continue offering something the market no longer values. Customer relationships therefore require both appreciation and continuous attention.
>
> <!-- word_comment_id: 531; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: Topic: Customers in business -->
>
> </details>

>

> **Topic: Team motivation**
>
> <!-- audio_source_key: listening-p4-set028-audio -->
> <!-- transcript_status: source_word_comment -->
> <!-- word_comment_ids: 532 -->
> <details class="audio-transcript">
> <summary>Audio transcript</summary>
>
> When a team's project falls behind schedule, the visible problem may be a missed deadline, but the psychological effect can be more serious. Team members often lose confidence. They begin their own judgement and may hesitate before making decisions that previously felt routine. They do not usually become more assured, and starting the entire project again is rarely the first response. A leader must therefore rebuild belief without pretending that the setback did not happen. Some managers increase the budget or monitor each person more closely, but money and surveillance do not automatically restore motivation. In my experience, the most effective step is to set a realistic goal. An attainable objective gives the team evidence that progress is possible and creates a clear next action. Focusing only on individual performance can damage cooperation, while an unnecessarily large budget may introduce pressure without solving the real issue. Once the group reaches a realistic milestone, confidence begins to return, and more demanding goals can be introduced gradually. Motivation grows when people can see a credible path forward.
>
> <!-- word_comment_id: 532; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: Topic: Team motivation -->
>
> </details>

> **Topic: Travelling and Reading**

> My brother travels frequently, but not because he wants to escape his job or produce impressive photographs for social media. He finds travelling meaningful because it brings him joy and gives him opportunities to learn about different cultures. He stays with local families when possible, attends community events, and asks about ordinary routines rather than visiting only famous attractions. The value lies in seeing how other people organise their lives and reconsidering assumptions he previously treated as universal. In that respect, travelling is similar to reading a book. Both can help people discover new worlds and grow as a person. A novel may introduce an unfamiliar society through imagination, while a journey provides direct experience, but each expands perspective. Neither activity is mandatory for academic success, and reading certainly does not require the physical endurance of a cycling expedition. What they share is the ability to move someone beyond a narrow personal viewpoint. After a thoughtful book or a meaningful trip, the world often appears more complex, and the individual returns with new rather than simple answers.
>

> **Topic: A nutritionist's career**

> People sometimes assume I became a nutritionist after working in a hospital or after taking a position at a health centre. My path began earlier. I became a nutritionist while completing my biology degree, when a research project on diet and metabolism convinced me to specialise. The degree provided the scientific foundation, and I later gained practical experience in clinical settings, but those jobs were not the moment the career began. Public recognition came in an unexpected way. It was not the result of inventing a new food, and I had not yet appeared on a famous television programme. I became well known after helping people solve dietary problems through a telephone helpline. Callers asked about allergies, weight management, and affordable meals, and my explanations were shared widely because they were practical and easy to understand. Television invitations followed later. The experience taught me that professional reputation can grow from useful public service rather than from publicity alone. People remembered the advice because it addressed real problems in their everyday lives.

> **Topic: Viral video**

> A recent online video became extremely popular, and many observers assumed that celebrities or a huge marketing budget must have been responsible. Neither explanation is accurate. What makes the video stand out is its production technique. The camera movement appears effortless, the editing creates a surprising change of perspective, and the sound is timed so precisely that viewers often replay the clip to understand how it was made. These technical choices give a simple idea the of a professional short film. Strong production alone, however, does not guarantee that a video will spread. According to the reviewer, videos become more successful when they are released at the right time. This particular clip appeared during a public conversation that made its theme immediately relevant. It did not follow a long-term trend mechanically, and translation into several languages was not necessary because the story was mainly visual. Online success combines with timing: creators need something distinctive, but they must also understand when an audience is most ready to notice, discuss, and share it.

>

> **Topic: Business strategy**
>
> <!-- audio_source_key: listening-p4-set032-audio -->
> <!-- transcript_status: source_word_comment -->
> <!-- word_comment_ids: 536 -->
> <details class="audio-transcript">
> <summary>Audio transcript</summary>
>
> Identifying the correct market is one of the first serious decisions a company makes. The benefit is not that competitors disappear or that lower standards become acceptable. A clear target market helps the company focus resources and avoid wasting time on areas where demand is weak. Staff can design relevant products, choose suitable communication channels, and direct research toward customers who are genuinely likely to buy. Without that focus, even a large budget may be spread too thinly to achieve useful results. Profitability is equally important, but it is often misunderstood as a sign that research and competitive analysis are no longer necessary. In fact, profitability proves that a company can cover its costs and strengthen its position in the market. It provides money for improvement, resilience during difficult periods, and future investment. It does not allow managers to ignore competitors. A profitable firm must still monitor change and protect the reasons customers choose it. Market selection and profitability therefore support each other: one concentrates effort, while the other confirms that the strategy can survive financially.
>
> <!-- word_comment_id: 536; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: Topic: Business strategy -->
>
> </details>

>

>
> B. It guarantees that the business will operate without any rival firms.
>
> C. It provides a justification for lowering product standards to maximize profit.
>

>

>
> C. It suggests that the company can disregard the activities of its competitors.

> **Topic: Industry of Making Music (2026)**
>
> <!-- audio_source_key: listening-p4-set033-audio -->
> <!-- transcript_status: source_word_comment -->
> <!-- word_comment_ids: 537 -->
> <details class="audio-transcript">
> <summary>Audio transcript</summary>
>
> A long career in music depends on more than winning awards or collecting a large number of fans. Audiences change, fashions disappear, and early publicity can fade very The key is an artist's belief in her musical instincts. A performer who understands her own sound can adapt without copying every temporary trend. That confidence does not mean refusing advice; it means knowing which suggestions support the music and which would weaken its identity. The current industry makes that confidence difficult to develop because performers find it hard to get started. Digital platforms allow anyone to upload a song, but they also create overwhelming competition. New musicians must attract attention among thousands of releases while learning production, promotion, and live performance at the same time. It is not easier than ever to become genuinely established, and most artists are not simply returning to traditional styles. Success may arrive suddenly online, but building a sustainable career still requires opportunity, patience, and a clear creative direction. Trusting musical judgement helps an artist continue when early recognition is limited.
>
> <!-- word_comment_id: 537; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: Topic: Industry of Making Music (2026) -->
>
> </details>

> **Topic: Productivity (2026)**
>
> <!-- audio_source_key: listening-p4-set034-audio -->
> <!-- transcript_status: source_word_comment -->
> <!-- word_comment_ids: 538 -->
> <details class="audio-transcript">
> <summary>Audio transcript</summary>
>
> People searching for greater productivity often respond by extending the working day. Longer hours can increase output briefly, but fatigue eventually reduces and creates mistakes. The most reliable way to maximise productivity is effective time management. This involves identifying priorities, dividing large tasks into manageable stages, and protecting time for work that requires concentration. Planning does not need to remove flexibility; it should make deliberate change easier. The difficulty is that productivity strategies require effort to use effectively. A calendar, task system, or weekly review produces no benefit if it is ignored after several days. These methods are not limited to students, and most can be implemented without expensive equipment. The challenge is consistency: people must review commitments, say no to lower-value activity, and continue using a system even when work becomes busy. Productivity is therefore not a clever trick that removes effort. Good management directs effort toward the right task and prevents energy from being lost through confusion, repetition, or constant switching.
>
> <!-- word_comment_id: 538; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: Topic: Productivity (2026) -->
>
> </details>

>
> The new podcast contained thoughtful interviews, and the episodes were not unusually long. Its failure came from a different source: the creators did not promote the show effectively. They uploaded each episode and assumed listeners would discover it automatically, but they rarely shared short clips, contacted related communities, or explained clearly what made the programme distinctive. As a result, even people who might have enjoyed the content did not know it existed. This example should not lead us to dismiss podcasting. In my view, it is a great way to build a community. Regular episodes allow hosts and listeners to develop an ongoing conversation, especially when audiences can send , suggest topics, or respond to one another. Podcasting is not only useful for entertainment; it can support education, professional discussion, and shared interests. Nor is it a waste of time when the purpose and audience are clear. The lesson is that good content and community potential are not enough by themselves. Creators must also make the programme visible and invite people into the conversation.

> **Topic: Making Plans (2026)**
>
> <!-- audio_source_key: listening-p4-set037-audio -->
> <!-- transcript_status: source_word_comment -->
> <!-- word_comment_ids: 541 -->
> <details class="audio-transcript">
> <summary>Audio transcript</summary>
>
> Planning is useful because it helps people make decisions. When several options appear, a plan provides priorities against which each choice can be judged. It does not make life completely predictable, and it certainly does not reduce the need to communicate with other people. Instead, it gives a reason for choosing one action over another and prevents every new request from becoming an emergency. The difficulty is that planning requires you to set yourself certain limits. Time, money, and attention are finite, so accepting one commitment usually means refusing or delaying another. Some people dislike this because limits can feel restrictive, but without them a plan becomes a list of wishes rather than a workable guide. Planning does not only apply in professional situations, and it need not discourage all risk. A good plan can include experimentation and revision. The important point is to recognise constraints honestly, choose what matters most, and update the plan when circumstances change. Decisions become clearer when priorities and limits are visible.
>
> <!-- word_comment_id: 541; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: Topic: Making Plans (2026) -->
>
> </details>

>

>
> C. They reduce the need for communication.
>
<!-- source_key: listening-p4-set037-q002 -->

>

>

> **Topic: The Sport (Version 2) (2026)**
>
> <!-- audio_source_key: listening-p4-set038-audio -->
> <!-- transcript_status: source_word_comment -->
> <!-- word_comment_ids: 542 -->
> <details class="audio-transcript">
> <summary>Audio transcript</summary>
>
> Debates about school sport often focus on health, but organised competition can teach another kind of lesson. In my view, sports competitions prepare students for a competitive work environment. Young people experience pressure, learn to recover after disappointment, and discover that preparation influences performance. This is not primarily about attracting international sponsorship or selecting athletes for national teams. The educational value lies in learning how to compete responsibly without treating one result as a judgement of personal worth. Schools should nevertheless avoid allowing competition to dominate student life. My broader advice is that school sport should provide students with balance in their lives. Academic subjects demand concentration and individual assessment, whereas physical activity offers movement, teamwork, and a different source of achievement. I am not recommending a strict graduation requirement for every student, and restricting activity to protect academic performance would remove that benefit. A thoughtful programme includes competitive opportunities while also offering inclusive participation. Students need both challenge and recovery, and sport can support that balance when success is defined more widely than winning.
>
> <!-- word_comment_id: 542; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: Topic: The Sport (Version 2) (2026) -->
>
> </details>

>

> C. Identify and recruit professional athletes for national teams.
>
<!-- source_key: listening-p4-set038-q002 -->

>
> B. Enforce athletic participation as a strict graduation requirement.

> **Topic: The Sport (Version 3) (2026)**
>
> <!-- audio_source_key: listening-p4-set039-audio -->
> <!-- transcript_status: source_word_comment -->
> <!-- word_comment_ids: 543 -->
> <details class="audio-transcript">
> <summary>Audio transcript</summary>
>
> School sport is sometimes discussed only in terms of winning matches, but its most direct benefit is broader: it helps promote students' overall health. Regular physical activity supports fitness, sleep, concentration, and emotional well-being. It is not designed to reduce physical effort, and its purpose should not be confused with academic competition. Students who move regularly may actually return to classroom work with greater energy. Sport also provides balance. School life can become dominated by examinations, homework, and hours spent sitting. Training or playing with a team introduces movement, social contact, and a different form of progress. That does not mean students should specialise at an extreme level or place individual success above the group. The strongest programmes allow different abilities to participate and connect physical development with cooperation. Balance is achieved when sport supports study rather than competing with it for every available hour. Schools should therefore treat physical activity as part of a healthy educational experience, not merely as preparation for elite competition.
>

> **Topic: Television Series (Version 3) (2026)**

>
> The drama series North Harbour had experienced actors and a promising subject, but the premiere failed to engage the audience from the start. The opening episode introduced too many unexplained events, and viewers had little reason to care about the central character before the first major crisis occurred. The failure was not caused by an enormous celebrity marketing budget, nor was the story adapted from a famous novel. The problem was the confusing beginning. Later episodes improved, yet the broadcaster created another difficulty by promoting the programme everywhere. Trailers appeared repeatedly between shows, interviews filled entertainment pages, and plot images circulated long before episodes were shown. The reviewer argues that series are damaged by overexposure. When audiences see the same material constantly, curiosity can turn into irritation, and surprises lose their effect. Inconsistent production or limited casting diversity may be valid concerns in other programmes, but they are not the criticism made here. A series needs enough promotion to be noticed, but not so much that viewers feel exhausted before the story has a chance to develop.
>

> Advertising remains important even in a world of recommendations and social media. A well-designed campaign helps to reach new customers by introducing a product to people outside the company's existing audience. Its main purpose is not to reduce production costs, and digital media has not made advertising automatically ineffective. The channels have changed, but businesses still need a way to become visible. Sponsorship in sport is more complicated. It can provide money and attention, yet advertising arrangements are not always good for sports fans. Commercial breaks may interrupt events, ticketing decisions may favour sponsors, and excessive branding can make supporters feel that tradition has been replaced by marketing. Sponsorship certainly does not guarantee that a team will win, and major campaigns are not limited to local amateur events. The expert's point is not that all advertising should disappear from sport. Rather, organisers should ask whether commercial activity improves the experience or begins to control it. Financial support is valuable, but fans, athletes, and the identity of the sport should remain more important than the sponsor's demand for visibility.

> **Topic: Regional Development Plan (Version 2) (2026)**

> The revised Regional Development Plan proposes new housing and road capacity, but one major criticism remains: it does not provide enough alternatives to driving. Several neighbourhoods would receive wider roads, yet the plan contains only limited bus improvements and disconnected cycle routes. The objection is not about international loans or an exclusive focus on cities rather than rural areas. Residents want practical options that reduce dependence on private cars. This weakness is likely to create a second challenge. The plan may meet resistance from local communities, particularly those facing increased traffic, construction, or the loss of open land without receiving better public transport in return. The legal framework has already been established, and no confirmed shortage of engineers has been identified. Public meetings show that opposition is based on the content of the proposal rather than a technical delay. If planners want support, they will need to consult affected neighbourhoods and demonstrate that walking, cycling, and public transport are genuine parts of the design. Ignoring those concerns could slow approval even if the engineering work is possible.
>

> The latest novel is uneven, but its plot is very strong. Events are carefully connected, tension increases at the right moments, and the final chapters resolve details introduced much earlier. This narrative control is what makes the ending feel complete; it was not mainly the result of advice from a famous editor, and the book was not simply finished faster than previous work. The may be awkwardly expressed, but the speaker's judgement is clear: the strength lies in the plot. Some readers nevertheless criticise the novel for being very similar to the author's other books. The setting has changed and several names are new, yet the central conflict, character types, and final revelation follow a familiar pattern. Their complaint is not about academic vocabulary or the physical of the hardback edition. Loyal fans may enjoy recognising the author's usual formula, while other readers want greater risk and originality. The novel therefore succeeds as a controlled story but may not convince those hoping to see a genuinely new direction.
>
> <!-- word_comment_id: 547; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: Topic: Criticism Of The New Novel (Version 2) (2026) -->

> <!-- audio_source_key: listening-p4-set044-audio -->

> <details class="audio-transcript">

> The novel has attracted attention for its ambitious setting, but many readers struggle to connect with the characters. The problem is not that there are too many central figures or that their motivations are explained too clearly. Rather, the characters are difficult to relate to. Their reactions often feel distant from ordinary experience, and important emotional decisions happen without enough believable preparation. Readers understand what the characters do, yet they do not always understand them as people. The storyline has also received criticism because it lacks originality. The novel uses a familiar missing-person mystery, a secret family history, and a final revelation that resembles several recent bestsellers. The plot is not especially complex for a general audience, and experts have not mainly complained that every twist is predictable. Their broader concern is that the story depends too heavily on established patterns. Stronger character development might still have made the familiar structure satisfying, but the combination of distant characters and an unoriginal storyline prevents the novel from creating the emotional impact its setting initially promises.
>

> **Topic: Advertising and sponsoring (Version 2) (2026)**
>
> <!-- audio_source_key: listening-p4-set045-audio -->
> <!-- transcript_status: source_word_comment -->
> <!-- word_comment_ids: 549 -->
> <details class="audio-transcript">
> <summary>Audio transcript</summary>
>
> Advertising is sometimes criticised as an unnecessary expense, but its basic importance is straightforward: it can help a business reach new customers. Brand recognition may also improve, yet that is not the specific benefit being emphasised here, and advertising does not automatically increase the price of every product. A campaign introduces an offer to people who might never encounter it through ordinary sales channels. Sponsoring sport, however, requires caution. A company may expect the popularity of a tournament to improve its own image, but the association works in both directions. If an event becomes connected with cheating, violence, corruption, or poor organisation, sponsorship can generate negative publicity for the sport. The main concern is not simply that tournaments are expensive or that sponsorship creates unfair competition. The reputational relationship itself is the risk. Businesses and organisers should examine values, governance, and possible public reaction before signing an agreement. Financial support can benefit athletes and fans, but a poorly chosen partnership may leave both the company and the sport remembered for the wrong reasons.
>

> **Topic: Work And Books**

> <!-- audio_source_key: listening-p4-set046-audio -->

> Work provides income, but its contribution to an individual can be much wider. Through work, people develop skills, contribute to society, and gain a sense of purpose. A useful role can strengthen confidence and create relationships rather than serving as a way to avoid social contact. Nor should employment be understood mainly as a method for maximising weekend leisure. The value lies in applying knowledge and effort to something that matters beyond immediate payment. Books support a different but complementary kind of development. Reading introduces ideas, experiences, and perspectives that may not appear in a person's daily job. When work and books are balanced, the result can be growth in every area of a person's life. Professional experience tests ideas in practice, while reading encourages reflection and imagination. This balance does not require a complete separation of private and professional interests, and it does not make formal education unnecessary. Instead, the two activities reinforce each other. A thoughtful reader may become a more adaptable worker, and practical experience may deepen the meaning found in books.

> <!-- audio_source_key: listening-p4-set047-audio -->
> <!-- transcript_status: source_word_comment -->

> <details class="audio-transcript">
> <summary>Audio transcript</summary>
>
> A striking of many modern works is that they have something to say about modern society. Rather than following classical artistic conventions without or concentrating only on historical figures, contemporary writers and artists examine technology, inequality, identity, isolation, and changing relationships. Their work can act as a response to the world in which audiences currently live. That relevance does not mean the message is always obvious. In fact, the meanings of these works are often not easily identified. Artists may use symbolism, fragmented structure, or an unreliable point of view instead of giving a direct moral lesson. The core message is rarely stated clearly in the opening. Viewers and readers have to connect images, notice contrasts, and consider why familiar situations are presented in an unusual way. Some people find this indirectness frustrating, while others value the freedom to interpret. The difficulty is not necessarily a weakness. It can encourage audiences to discuss the work and recognise that modern social problems often do not have simple explanations or one universally accepted conclusion.
>
> <!-- word_comment_id: 551; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: Topic: Modern Society -->
>
> </details>

>

>
> C. Focus exclusively on historical events and figures
>
<!-- source_key: listening-p4-set047-q002 -->

>
> <u>A. The meanings are not easily identified</u>
>
> B. They provide direct and simplified moral lessons
>

> **Topic: The importance of sleep (Version 2) (2026)**
>
> <!-- audio_source_key: listening-p4-set048-audio -->
> <!-- transcript_status: source_word_comment -->
> <!-- word_comment_ids: 552 -->
> <details class="audio-transcript">
> <summary>Audio transcript</summary>
>
> Several popular beliefs about sleep are misleading. Drinking coffee before bed does not generally relax the brain, and deliberately sleeping fewer hours is not a reliable way to improve deep sleep. According to the speaker, blocking out noise and light is key. A , dark room reduces stimulation and gives the body a more consistent signal that it is time to rest. Comfort and routine matter, but the sensory environment is the clearest recommendation. Insomnia is also misunderstood. It is not caused only by physical health problems, and people with insomnia do not usually sleep too much. One important difficulty is that people cannot always recognise the symptoms of tiredness. They may continue working, driving, or using a phone because they have become accustomed to poor concentration and low energy. As a result, they fail to respond to the body's need for recovery and may make the sleep problem worse. Recognising subtle signs of fatigue is therefore part of managing insomnia, not merely counting the number of hours spent in bed.
>
> <!-- word_comment_id: 552; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: Topic: The importance of sleep (Version 2) (2026) -->
>
> </details>

>
<!-- source_key: listening-p4-set048-q001 -->

> <u>A. Blocking out noise and light is key</u>

>
> C. Sleeping fewer hours increases deep sleep quality
>
<!-- source_key: listening-p4-set048-q002 -->
> **16.2. What is the speaker's view on insomnia in general?**
>
> <u>A. People can't always recognize the symptoms of tiredness</u>
>
> B. Most people with insomnia actually sleep too much
>
> C. Insomnia is caused only by physical health problems

> **Topic: Cycling expedition**
>
> <!-- audio_source_key: listening-p4-set050-audio -->
> <!-- transcript_status: source_word_comment -->
> <!-- word_comment_ids: 554 -->
> <details class="audio-transcript">
> <summary>Audio transcript</summary>
>
> A long-distance cycling expedition needs physical preparation, suitable equipment, and emergency supplies, but my first advice is to be well informed about the places you are going to visit. Research road conditions, border rules, local transport, weather patterns, and access to accommodation before leaving. Carrying extra food and water can be sensible, and rough terrain may require caution, yet neither is a complete substitute for understanding the route. My broader advice is to learn about the people and cultures in the different countries. A cyclist moves slowly enough to enter small communities that ordinary tourists may pass without noticing. Knowing basic customs, respectful behaviour, and a few local expressions can prevent misunderstandings and create warmer encounters. I would not restrict the journey only to regions with perfect weather, and travelling at your own pace does not mean ignoring the destination or local context. A successful expedition is not measured only by distance. It is also shaped by how safely and respectfully the traveller interacts with the places through which the route passes.
>
> <!-- word_comment_id: 554; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: Topic: Cycling expedition -->
>
> </details>

>
<!-- source_key: listening-p4-set050-q001 -->

>
> B. Always carry extra water and food supplies.
>
> C. Avoid cycling on rough terrain.
>
<!-- source_key: listening-p4-set050-q002 -->
> **16.2. What is the speaker's advice in general?**
>
> <u>A. Knowing the people and cultures in the different countries.</u>
>
> B. Only visit places with good weather conditions.
>
> C. Cycle at your own pace without worrying about the destination.

> **Topic: Biography (2026)**
>
> <!-- audio_source_key: listening-p4-set051-audio -->
> <!-- transcript_status: source_word_comment -->
> <!-- word_comment_ids: 555 -->
> <details class="audio-transcript">
> <summary>Audio transcript</summary>
>
> This biography's greatest strength is its detailed description of a specific period of time. The author reconstructs the subject's city, working conditions, political tensions, and ordinary routines so carefully that readers understand not only what happened but also why certain choices were possible in that historical moment. The book is not especially short, and it does not focus only on already famous people. Much of its value comes from the wider world surrounding the central figure. Critics have also described it as an unconventional biography. Instead of presenting events in a simple chronological sequence, the writer moves between letters, court records, memories, and imagined reconstructions clearly identified as interpretation. Some reviewers admire this method because it reveals uncertainty rather than pretending every detail is known. They do not mainly say that the book is too difficult to understand or that it contains an excessive amount of fictional material. Its unusual structure challenges traditional biography while remaining grounded in evidence. That combination makes the historical period feel vivid and open to debate.
>

>
> </details>

> **Topic: Managing financial spending (Version 2) (2026)**
>
> <!-- audio_source_key: listening-p4-set052-audio -->
> <!-- transcript_status: source_word_comment -->
> <!-- word_comment_ids: 556 -->
> <details class="audio-transcript">
> <summary>Audio transcript</summary>
>
> To control short-term spending, begin by monitoring what you spend and using that information to create a weekly plan. Small purchases are easy to forget, so recording food, transport, subscriptions, and entertainment reveals where money is actually going. Credit cards do not automatically control spending, and trying to save a large sum every single day is usually unrealistic. A weekly plan creates a practical limit while allowing adjustments between categories. Long-term decisions require a different kind of support. People should seek advice from someone who has experience, such as a financial adviser or a trusted person who has successfully managed similar goals. Keeping every saving in one account may not suit different time horizons, and avoiding long-term planning leaves major expenses unprepared. Experienced guidance can help someone compare risk, understand fees, and avoid decisions based only on fear or excitement. Financial control therefore combines close observation in the short term with informed advice for the future. Neither method removes personal responsibility, but both make decisions more deliberate and easier to review.
>
> <!-- word_comment_id: 556; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: Topic: Managing financial spending (Version 2) (2026) -->
>
> </details>

>
<!-- source_key: listening-p4-set052-q001 -->

>
> <u>A. Monitor your spending for a weekly plan</u>
>
> B. Rely on credit cards to control spending
>
> C. Put aside a large sum every single day only
>
<!-- source_key: listening-p4-set052-q002 -->

>
> <u>A. Seek advice from someone who have experience</u>
>
> B. Keep all your savings in a single account
>
> C. Avoid making any long-term financial plans

> **Topic: Television Series (2026)**
>
> <!-- audio_source_key: listening-p4-set053-audio -->
> <!-- transcript_status: source_word_comment -->
> <!-- word_comment_ids: 557 -->
> <details class="audio-transcript">
> <summary>Audio transcript</summary>
>
> Successful television series usually have more than a famous cast. One major strength is that they maintain consistent throughout. Viewers may forgive a weaker scene, but large changes in writing, acting, or production from one episode to the next can damage trust. Series are not automatically successful because they are shorter than films; many require a much greater total time commitment. Consistency allows audiences to believe that continued viewing will be rewarded. Television production is also changing because viewer habits influence the way series are made. Streaming audiences may watch several episodes in one evening, pause for weeks, or discover a programme long after its original broadcast. Writers and producers now consider episode length, opening pace, recap structure, and the timing of major revelations in response to those habits. Streaming platforms may affect costs, but reduced cost is not the main change described, and the speaker does not simply claim that television is becoming less creative. The form is adapting to how, when, and where people choose to watch.
>
> <!-- word_comment_id: 557; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: Topic: Television Series (2026) -->
>
> </details>

> **Topic: Script production (2026)**

> <summary>Audio transcript</summary>
>
> The main weakness of this script is that the characters' backgrounds are not adequately explored. We see them making major decisions, but we learn too little about their previous relationships, fears, and experiences to understand those choices. The setting is believable, and the dialogue is not especially complex; the emotional problem comes from missing context. Without a stronger sense of who the characters were before the story began, viewers may observe the action without becoming deeply involved. The speaker also criticises the effect of the industry on writing. New industry demands have a negative influence on script production. Tight deadlines, pressure to follow current trends, and repeated commercial revisions can reduce the time available for research and character development. These demands do not automatically increase audience engagement or provide writers with greater creative freedom. They may instead encourage safe, produced material. Improving this script therefore requires more than changing a few lines. Writers need time to build convincing personal histories and connect those histories naturally to the events shown on screen.
>

> **Topic: A Promotion Campaign for a Product (2026)**
>
> <!-- audio_source_key: listening-p4-set056-audio -->
> <!-- transcript_status: source_word_comment -->
> <!-- word_comment_ids: 560 -->
> <details class="audio-transcript">
> <summary>Audio transcript</summary>
>
> My main criticism of this promotion campaign is that it makes exaggerated claims about the product. The advertisements promise immediate and dramatic results without presenting enough evidence, which may attract attention briefly but can weaken trust once customers compare the promise with reality. The problem is not a shortage of information or excessive focus on a low price. The language itself creates expectations that the product is unlikely to meet. There is also a financial concern. The campaign may not be profitable because it costs too much to make compared with the possible return. Celebrity fees, expensive video production, and repeated paid advertising have consumed a large budget, while projected sales remain uncertain. The campaign has already attracted public attention, and the target group appears appropriate, so those are not the main reasons for doubt. A simpler, more credible campaign might produce a better return and protect the brand's reputation. Promotion should make a product visible, but spending and promises must remain proportionate to what the product can realistically deliver.
>
> <!-- word_comment_id: 560; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: Topic: A Promotion Campaign for a Product (2026) -->
>
> </details>

>

>

The new biography is similar to the previous book about the scientist, which was also a bestseller. It follows a familiar structure, combines personal history with clear scientific explanation, and addresses many of the readers enjoyed in the earlier volume. Being similar does not mean every chapter is repeated. New letters and interviews provide additional material, but the tone and approach remain recognisable. On the contrary, readers who trusted the style of the earlier biography know what kind of clear, engaging account to expect. Familiar accessibility has helped the new work reach a broad audience

> **Topic: Famous writers (Version 2) (2026)**
>
> <!-- audio_source_key: listening-p4-set058-audio -->
> <!-- transcript_status: source_word_comment -->
> <!-- word_comment_ids: 562 -->
> <details class="audio-transcript">
> <summary>Audio transcript</summary>
>
> The two writers discussed today came from different countries and did not always write in the same genre. Their most interesting connection is that they both make references to each other in their work. One author adapts an image introduced by the other, while the second responds indirectly through an essay and a later fictional character. They were not born in the same place, and shared genre is not the reason they are compared. Their works also have a distinctive difficulty: the meaning is not always easily identified. Both writers use symbols, indirect narration, and hidden connections that reward careful reading. The stories are not all based on real events, and their language and structure cannot generally be described as simple. A reader may follow the surface plot while missing the intellectual conversation taking place between the two authors. Recognising their references can therefore change the interpretation of an entire passage. This combination of mutual influence and layered meaning explains why scholars continue comparing them and why new readers often discover additional ideas on a second or third reading.
>
> <!-- word_comment_id: 562; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: Topic: Famous writers (Version 2) (2026) -->
>
> </details>

> **Topic: Life after university (Version 2) (2026)**
>
> <!-- audio_source_key: listening-p4-set059-audio -->
> <!-- transcript_status: source_word_comment -->
> <!-- word_comment_ids: 563 -->
> <details class="audio-transcript">
> <summary>Audio transcript</summary>
>
> Choosing a career after university can feel urgent, but students should be flexible and open-minded. Their first role may introduce an industry they had not considered, and skills gained in one position may later lead somewhere unexpected. Following friends into the same career or focusing only on salary can prevent graduates from noticing opportunities that better match their strengths. Flexibility does not mean accepting every offer; it means exploring possibilities before making a narrow long-term commitment. Applicants must also recognise that jobs are becoming more competitive. Many people may hold similar degrees, so employers increasingly compare experience, communication, digital skills, and evidence of initiative. It is not true that most jobs have stopped requiring , and handwritten applications are not a general preference. The practical lesson is to prepare carefully and show a distinctive combination of abilities. Graduates should research organisations, adapt applications to each role, and continue learning after formal education ends. Open-minded career choices and realistic awareness of competition make the transition into employment more manageable.
>
> <!-- word_comment_id: 563; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: Topic: Life after university (Version 2) (2026) -->
>
> </details>

> **Topic: Exhibition by Greek artist Jame Panos (2026)**
>
> <!-- audio_source_key: listening-p4-set060-audio -->
> <!-- transcript_status: source_word_comment -->
> <!-- word_comment_ids: 564 -->
> <details class="audio-transcript">
> <summary>Audio transcript</summary>
>
> Good afternoon, everyone, and welcome to the National Gallery. Today, I'd like to introduce our new exhibition by the Greek artist Jame Panos. The exhibition includes more than thirty paintings, most of which were completed during the last ten years of his career. Panos is well known for his and emotional style. His paintings often show empty rooms, lonely figures, dark streets, and old buildings under grey skies. Because of this, many art critics have said that his paintings would be characterized by sadness. However, this sadness is not expressed in a dramatic way. Instead, it appears gently, through soft colours, simple shapes, and a strong sense of silence. One of the most interesting things about this exhibition is the way Panos uses memory. Several paintings were inspired by the small village in Greece where he grew up. The village has changed a lot since his childhood, and many of the places shown in the paintings no longer exist. For visitors, the exhibition feels like a window into the past, allowing them to see not only the artist's personal memories, but also a disappearing part of Greek life. The exhibition will be open until the end of next month. Guided tours are available every Friday and Sunday afternoon.
>
> <!-- word_comment_id: 564; author: APTIS Transcript; anchor: Topic: Exhibition by Greek artist Jame Panos (2026) -->
>
> </details>

>

> <u>B. They would be characterized by sadness.</u>
>
> C. They mainly focused on modern city life.
>
<!-- source_key: listening-p4-set060-q002 -->
